jwatson on DSKGBHNXB4PROD with HOUSE

PRAYER
The Chaplain, the Reverend Margaret
Grun Kibben, offered the following
prayer:
God of justice and peace, mercy and
love, at this 11th hour on this 11th day
of the 11th month, we pause in prayer
to You in gratitude for their service,
giving thanks for the countless men
and women, many of them our own
family members and friends, who willingly risked their lives in our Nation’s
conflicts to protect and defend the
freedom and democracy of these United
States.
Greater love has no one than these
Americans who demonstrated the full
measure of devotion by their willingness to lay down their lives for their
countrymen. There is no greater sacrifice than that which our veterans
have made in service to this Nation.
Heal the wounds of war that still
plague them, both visible and invisible.
Apply Your balm of comfort on the
battle scars that yet riddle their bodies
and their souls. Grant them the peace
they strove for as they reckon their
spirits with the untold cost of their
service.
And inspire us not just to thank
them for their service, but to welcome

their stories, to seek their wisdom, and
to value the contributions they continue to make for the betterment of
our communities.
God, with lasting gratitude, we honor
the brave who have allowed us to live
in this land of the free.
In Your eternal name we pray.
Amen.
f

Speaker, under the progrowth agenda

of President Donald Trump and House
Republicans, led by Speaker MIKE
JOHNSON, America grew at an annual
rate of 3.4 percent in the third quarter,
the strongest pace in a year.
President Trump has an America
First agenda delivering, as promised,
historic levels of investment and faster
growth, creating jobs.
Third quarter achievements are:
Private investment increased by 9.4
percent.
Business investment has grown by 13
percent.
Consumer spending grew by 3.8 percent.
South Carolina will continue record
growth with Governor-elect Alan Wilson and Lieutenant Governor Mike
Reichenbach, and all other statewide
State officials are Republicans.
In conclusion, God bless our troops.
Trump is reinstituting peace through
strength, revealing war criminal Putin
lies, insulting Trump, mocking Trump,
with Putin supporting the regime in
Yemen, achieving death to Israel,
death to America. President Vucic of
Serbia has been defeated in an election
last week, an ally of Putin.
As a father grateful for four servicemembers, on this Veterans Day, I am
grateful for all of these men and
women who have served our greatest
country in uniform.
God bless our troops. God bless our
veterans.

HONORING THE LIFE AND LEGACY
OF JOE CONVERTINI
(Ms. TENNEY asked and was given
permission to address the House for 1
minute and to revise and extend her remarks.)
Ms. TENNEY. Mr. Speaker, I rise to
recognize tomorrow, November 12, as
Creutzfeldt-Jakob Disease, or CJD,
Awareness Day.
I also rise to honor Joe Convertini, a
lifelong resident of Cortland, New
York, and former constituent, who
passed away from CJD this past August.
If you knew Joe, you were truly
blessed. I was privileged to get to know
Joe through his daughter Julia, who
served on my staff in the New York
State Assembly.
Joe was a devoted husband to his
wife of 38 years, Kellyann; a caring
brother to his younger brother, Phil;
and the most wonderful father to his
three beautiful girls, Victoria, Julia,
and Sophia.
Joe lived his life with grace, kindness, and integrity. He loved his home.
He loved God. He loved his family with
every fiber of his being.
On August 1, Joe’s life was cut short
by CJD, a disease that most Americans
have probably never heard of. This
rare, rapidly progressive brain disease
is not only terminal, but it is almost
impossible to detect. The initial symptoms are subtle and hardly noticeable
before it suddenly materializes into a
devastating neurological decline at remarkable speed.
For a month, his family was forced to
watch helplessly as Joe slipped away,
but the Convertini family’s experience
is not an anomaly. It is the cruel reality for many families with a loved
one afflicted by this disease.
While there is currently no treatment or cure, the CJD Foundation and
various other patient groups are working tirelessly to change that. Joe’s legacy will live on through his family’s
determination to fight for a cure. May
his memory move us to act.

his family.

CALLING FOR ENERGY PRICE
RELIEF
(Mr. LATIMER asked and was given
permission to address the House for 1
minute and to revise and extend his remarks.)
Mr. LATIMER. Mr. Speaker, as we
approach the winter months and the
weather gets colder, millions of Americans will see the cost of heating their
homes rise amidst the President’s war
in Iran.
According to the Joint Economic
Committee, the cost of home heating
oil in New York is estimated to increase by nearly 32 percent this winter.
At the same time, President Trump
has repeatedly called for eliminating
the Low-Income Home Energy Assistance Program, or LIHEAP, a popular
program that helps hardworking families afford to heat their homes.
Instead of delivering real price relief
for the American people, President
Trump’s war in Iran, his tariffs, and his
reckless cuts to social safety net programs are decimating family budgets
as we descend into the winter season.
Congress cannot leave these families
out in the cold. I implore my colleagues on both sides of the aisle to deliver real energy price relief before
families are forced to choose between
heating their homes and putting food
on their table.

HONORING BILL MAGNARELLI
(Mr. MANNION asked and was given
permission to address the House for 1
minute and to revise and extend his remarks.)
Mr. MANNION. Mr. Speaker, I rise
today to honor New York State Assemblyman Bill Magnarelli, a veteran himself, as he prepares to leave office after
14 terms and 28 remarkable years.
Born and raised in Syracuse, Bill is a
model public servant, delivering for
our businesses, schools, libraries, and
first responders.
He invested in our roads and bridges
and helped to not only restore but,
dare I say, save our beloved Landmark
Theatre. He brought the Central New
York Veterans Parade and Expo back
to life in 2008 and has led it ever since.
I was privileged to work with Bill in
Albany to give Syracuse Hancock
International Airport its own police
force, New York State’s first new police force in nearly 50 years.
Bill has been a mentor and friend to
me and to so many others. I thank Bill
for representing Syracuse and central
New York with such dedication and
honor. I wish him and his family all
the best.

had a 61-percent voter turnout, and almost 2 million ballots were cast.

which is the second-highest rate in a
century, just behind the 2018 midterms.

that he could stay in power.

People want to be able to afford gas
and groceries. They want us to stop engaging in illegal, immoral wars overseas. They want a path to the middle
class when we have greater income inequality than we have ever had.
It is time that we fight. The American people have made their voices
heard.

(Mr. DESAULNIER asked and was
given permission to address the House
for 1 minute and to revise and extend

Mr. DESAULNIER. Mr. Speaker, I
rise today to recognize Jane Garcia as
she retires from her role as the chief
executive officer of La Clinica.
Jane is a friend and a valued member
of the community I represent in the
San Francisco Bay Area.
Since Jane assumed her role in 1982,
La Clinica has provided culturally sensitive care to over 86,000 patients in the
east bay of the San Francisco Bay
Area.
An
advocate
for
accessible
healthcare, Jane has served on numerous boards and is president of the California Primary Care Association.
Among the many accolades that
Jane’s work has earned her, she was inducted into the Alameda County Women’s Hall of Fame. She has played an
invaluable role in La Clinica’s ability
to help marginalized communities
around the bay area and give them access to quality healthcare.
Please join me in congratulating my
friend, Jane Garcia, a valued member
of our community and a valued American, on her well-earned retirement.

COMBATING FOREIGN THREATS TO
MAIN STREET ACT OF 2026
Mr. WILLIAMS of Texas. Mr. Speaker, I move to suspend the rules and
pass the bill (H.R. 10329) to require the
Administrator of the Small Business

PO 00000

Frm 00004

Fmt 4634

Sfmt 0634

Administration to disseminate information on foreign adversary conduct
that poses material harm to small
business concerns, and for other purposes.
The Clerk read the title of the bill.
The text of the bill is as follows:
H.R. 10329
Be it enacted by the Senate and House of Representatives of the United States of America in
Congress assembled,
SECTION 1. SHORT TITLE.

This Act may be cited as the ‘‘Combating
Foreign Threats to Main Street Act of 2026’’.
SEC. 2. INFORMATION DISSEMINATION ON FOREIGN ADVERSARY CONDUCT THAT
POSES MATERIAL HARM TO SMALL
BUSINESS CONCERNS.
(a) EDUCATIONAL MATERIALS.—The Admin-

istrator of the Small Business Administration, in consultation with the appropriate
Federal agency heads, shall develop and disseminate educational materials regarding
foreign adversary conduct that poses material harm to small business concerns.
(b) DISSEMINATION.—The Administrator
shall make the educational materials described in subsection (a) available through
district offices and other programs of the Administration.
(c) CONTENTS.—The educational materials
described in subsection (a) shall, at a minimum—
(1) describe the nature of the foreign adversary conduct that poses material harm to
small business concerns;
(2) describe how such conduct materially
harms small business concerns;
(3) identify best practices for small business concerns to protect intellectual property, sensitive business information, and
supply chains;
(4) provide guidance regarding cybersecurity and data protection for small business
concerns;
(5) inform small business concerns of risks
associated with foreign investment, joint
ventures, licensing arrangements, and other
commercial relationships involving entities
owned or controlled by a foreign adversary
or an additional covered entity;
(6) describe resources available from the
Federal Government to help small business
concerns identify, avoid, prevent, and address such conduct; and
(7) provide guidance on safeguarding business interests in foreign markets without
unnecessarily restricting lawful commercial
activity.
(d) ANNUAL REVIEW AND UPDATES.—Beginning October 1, 2027, and annually thereafter,
the Administrator, in consultation with the
appropriate Federal agency heads, shall—
(1) review the educational materials described in subsection (a) to ensure such materials reflect emerging foreign adversary
conduct that poses material harm to small
business concerns, evolving tactics used by
foreign adversaries, changes in applicable
Federal law and policy, and current best
practices for protecting small business concerns; and
(2) update such materials as necessary to
remain accurate and up to date.
(e) NOTICE.—If, under subparagraph (F) of
subsection (f)(6), the Administrator determines, in consultation with the appropriate
Federal agency heads, that an activity poses
a significant risk to the economic or national security interests of the United
States, the Administrator shall notify the
Committee on Small Business of the House
of Representatives and the Committee on
Small Business and Entrepreneurship of the
Senate of the determination, what specific
activity has been determined to pose such a

E:\CR\FM\K11NO7.008

H11NOPT1

November 11, 2026

risk, and the justification for such determination of such activity.
(f) DEFINITIONS.—In this section:
(1) ADDITIONAL COVERED ENTITY.—The term
‘‘additional covered entity’’ means an entity,
an affiliate of an entity, or an individual included on a list described in section
9(g)(16)(D) of the Small Business Act (15
U.S.C. 638(g)(16)(D)).
(2) ADMINISTRATION.—The term ‘‘Administration’’ means the Small Business Administration.
(3) ADMINISTRATOR.—The term ‘‘Administrator’’ means the Administrator of the
Small Business Administration.
(4) APPROPRIATE FEDERAL AGENCY HEAD.—
The term ‘‘appropriate Federal agency head’’
means the Secretary of Homeland Security,
the Secretary of State, the Director of the
Federal Bureau of Investigation, the Secretary of the Treasury, the Secretary of
Commerce, the United States Trade Representative, the Director of the United
States Patent and Trademark Office, the Director of the United States Copyright Office,
and the head of any other relevant Federal
agency, as determined by the Administrator
of the Small Business Administration.
(5) FOREIGN ADVERSARY.—The term ‘‘foreign adversary’’ means a foreign adversary,
as defined in section 791.4 of title 15, Code of
Federal Regulations (or any successor regulation).
(6) FOREIGN ADVERSARY CONDUCT THAT
POSES MATERIAL HARM TO SMALL BUSINESS
CONCERNS.—The term ‘‘foreign adversary

conduct that poses material harm to small
business concerns’’ means an activity carried
out by a foreign adversary, an entity owned
or controlled by a foreign adversary, or an
additional covered entity that poses risks to
the economic or national security of the
United States or to small business concerns,
including—
(A) theft of intellectual property, trade secrets, or proprietary business information;
(B) cyber intrusions and malicious cyberenabled activities;
(C) forced labor and the importation of
goods produced wholly or in part with forced
labor;
(D) counterfeit goods, illicit trade, customs evasion, and related fraud;
(E) predatory or non-market investment
practices; and
(F) any other activity the Administrator
determines, in consultation with the appropriate Federal agency heads, poses a significant risk to the economic or national security interests of the United States.
(7) SMALL BUSINESS CONCERN.—The term
‘‘small business concern’’ has the meaning
given under section 3 of the Small Business
Act (15 U.S.C. 632).

Mr. Speaker, I rise today in strong
support of H.R. 10329, the Combating
Foreign Threats to Main Street Act of
2026. I introduced this bill alongside
the gentleman from California (Mr.
TRAN), my friend.
Whether it is a Fortune 500 company
or a small business on Main Street, no
American business is beyond the reach
of our foreign adversaries. China, Russia, and North Korea are working to
undermine our economic and national
security, and American businesses are
increasingly caught in the crosshairs.
The Chinese Communist Party, in
particular, is an expert at stealing intellectual property, exploiting unfair
trade practices, and using predatory investment to gain economic advantage
at our expense.
While these threats affect businesses
of every size, small businesses are often
the most vulnerable, and they simply
do not have the resources or expertise
to identify these threats and protect
themselves.
This bill closes that gap. It requires
the SBA to develop and publish educational materials on foreign adversary
conduct that poses material harm to
small businesses and their concerns.
These materials would also help
small businesses protect their intellectual property, sensitive information,
and supply chains. They will also help
small businesses understand the risks
of foreign investment, joint ventures,
and licensing arrangements tied to foreign adversaries.
The SBA must also review and update these materials annually to keep
pace with evolving threats. Congress
must be notified if the SBA identifies
such activity that poses a significant
risk to our economic and our national
security.
By strengthening awareness of foreign threats on Main Street, we can
better safeguard American innovation,
protect American jobs, and ensure that
our economic future is protected from
foreign adversaries.

joining me on this bill. I urge all my

DISASTER LOAN OUTREACH
CONSISTENCY ACT
Mr. WILLIAMS of Texas. Mr. Speaker, I move to suspend the rules and
pass the bill (H.R. 10355) to require the
Administrator of the Small Business

VerDate Sep 11 2014

November 11, 2026

CONGRESSIONAL RECORD — HOUSE

04:03 Nov 12, 2026

Jkt 079060

Administration to establish procedures
for consistency of certain disaster loan
outreach materials, and for other purposes.
The Clerk read the title of the bill.
The text of the bill is as follows:
H.R. 10355
Be it enacted by the Senate and House of Representatives of the United States of America in
Congress assembled,
SECTION 1. SHORT TITLE.

This Act may be cited as the ‘‘Disaster
Loan Outreach Consistency Act’’.
SEC. 2. PROCEDURES FOR CONSISTENCY OF DISASTER ASSISTANCE OUTREACH MATERIALS.
(a) OUTREACH MATERIALS.—Not later than

180 days after the date of the enactment of
this Act, the Administrator of the Small
Business Administration shall establish procedures to ensure that outreach materials,
including any press releases and fact sheets,
prepared by each disaster field operations
center of the Administration consistently include accurate and current information regarding—
(1) the terms of the disaster loan program
carried out under section 7(b) of the Small
Business Act (15 U.S.C. 636(b));
(2) any applicable changes to Federal disaster assistance eligibility processes relevant to applicants for such disaster loan
program; and
(3) such additional information, as determined by the Administrator.
(b) PROCEDURES.—The procedures established under subsection (a) shall include—
(1) standardized templates for outreach
materials;
(2) a process to verify, before public release, that outreach materials use the current templates and reflect current terms and
eligibility processes for the disaster loan
program;
(3) a review process to ensure consistency
in outreach materials and templates across
disaster field operations centers, where such
review occurs annually and immediately
after any significant changes to such procedures; and
(4) such additional procedures, as determined by the Administrator.
(c) NOTIFICATION.—Not later than 30 days
after establishing or making significant
changes to the procedures described in subsection (b), the Administrator shall submit
to the Committee on Small Business of the
House of Representatives and the Committee
on Small Business and Entrepreneurship of
the Senate a notice of such establishment.

Mr. Speaker, I rise today in support
of H.R. 10355, the Disaster Loan Outreach Consistency Act.
When disaster strikes—and it is
striking more frequently than ever before—it is crucial that victims have access to high-quality, up-to-date information about the resources available
to them. As these programs evolve, the
information and outreach related to
those programs must be updated to reflect the realities of the world we live
in.
The SBA’s disaster loan program,
which provides long-term, low-interest
loans to individuals, homeowners, and
businesses in the wake of a catastrophe, has seen several rule changes.
For instance, in 2023, SBA updated
the program to reflect cost-of-living increases. The following year, FEMA updated their programs to remove the requirement that people apply for the
disaster loan program as a precondition for some types of assistance.
These may sound minor, but they
make a material difference for disaster
victims.
Unfortunately, the GAO found that
not all victims were provided with full
information about the rule changes.
Specifically, some press releases contained different information depending
on which part of the country you lived.
As a result, people in the Eastern U.S.
often had more information about increased loan limits or the FEMA application process than people in the Western half of the United States.
The Disaster Loan Outreach Consistency Act simply codifies GAO recommendations that SBA establish a
process to ensure all disseminated information related to the disaster loan
program is current and consistent no
matter where you live.
I thank Representative MEJIA and
Delegate KING-HINDS for their bipartisan work in bringing this commonsense legislation forward.
Mr. Speaker, I urge my colleagues to
vote ‘‘yes,’’ and I yield back the balance of my time.

small businesses have the reliable information they need to access disaster
assistance and rebuild their communities.

of my time.

SBIC REPORTING MODERNIZATION
ACT OF 2026
Mr. WILLIAMS of Texas. Mr. Speaker, I move to suspend the rules and
pass the bill (H.R. 10353) to amend the
Small Business Investment Act of 1958
to require a quarterly report on small
business investment companies, and for
other purposes.
The Clerk read the title of the bill.
The text of the bill is as follows:
H.R. 10353
Be it enacted by the Senate and House of Representatives of the United States of America in
Congress assembled,
SECTION 1. SHORT TITLE.

This Act may be cited as the ‘‘SBIC Reporting Modernization Act of 2026’’.
SEC. 2. QUARTERLY SMALL BUSINESS INVESTMENT COMPANY PERFORMANCE REPORT.

Section 308(g) of the Small Business Investment Act of 1958 (15 U.S.C. 687(g)) is
amended by adding at the end the following
new paragraph:
‘‘(4) QUARTERLY REPORT.—
‘‘(A) IN GENERAL.—Beginning on the date
that is 90 days after the date of the enactment of this paragraph, and quarterly thereafter, the Administrator shall publish on a
webpage of the Administration a report on
small business investment companies licensed under this title.
‘‘(B) CONTENTS.—Each report required by
this paragraph shall include, for the period
covered by the report, the following:
‘‘(i) The information described in paragraph (3)(A).
‘‘(ii) The number of applicants approved by
the Administrator to proceed to the final licensing phase under section 107.300(b) of title
13, Code of Federal Regulations (or a similar
successor regulation).
‘‘(iii) The number of applicants received
the approval described in clause (ii) and met
the requirements of section 302(a).
‘‘(iv) The dollar value of debentures issued
under this title.
‘‘(v) The number of jobs created or sustained due to investments made by small
business investment companies.’’.

CYBERSECURITY FOR SMALL
BUSINESSES ACT OF 2026
Mr. WILLIAMS of Texas. Mr. Speaker, I move to suspend the rules and
pass the bill (H.R. 10238) to require the
Administrator of the Small Business
Administration to disseminate to small
business concerns certain information
and resources relating to cybersecurity
matters, and for other purposes, as
amended.
The Clerk read the title of the bill.
The text of the bill is as follows:
H.R. 10238
Be it enacted by the Senate and House of Representatives of the United States of America in
Congress assembled,
SECTION 1. SHORT TITLE.

This Act may be cited as the ‘‘Cybersecurity
for Small Businesses Act of 2026’’.
SEC. 2. INFORMATION AND RESOURCES RELATING TO CYBERSECURITY MATTERS
FOR SMALL BUSINESS CONCERNS.

The Administrator of the Small Business Administration, in consultation with the Director
of the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security
Agency and the Director of the National Institute of Standards and Technology, shall develop
and disseminate information and resources to
assist small business concerns in strengthening
the cybersecurity infrastructure of such concerns and implementing cybersecurity best practices.
SEC. 3. CYBERSECURITY COMPLIANCE INFORMATION.
(a) CYBERSECURITY MATURITY MODEL CERTIFICATION RESOURCES.—The Administrator of

the Small Business Administration, in coordination with the Secretary of Defense and other appropriate Federal agencies, shall provide information and resources to a small business concern seeking to enter into a contract with the

E:\CR\FM\K11NO7.019

H11NOPT1

H6158

Federal Government, or seeking to be a subcontractor on a Federal contract, regarding the Cybersecurity Maturity Model Certification program (or successor program), including information and resources produced and provided by—
(1) the Director of Small and Disadvantaged
Business Utilization (established under section
15(k) of the Small Business Act (15 U.S.C.
644(k))) for the Department of Defense;
(2) the program established under section 1807
of the National Defense Authorization Act for
Fiscal Year 2026 (10 U.S.C. 144 note); or
(3) the APEX Accelerator Program of the Department of Defense (or a successor program).
(b) PUBLICATION.—The Administrator shall include the information described in subsection (a)
in outreach and communications of the Small
Business Administration, including through
publication on a website of the Administration.
(c) BEST PRACTICES.—The Administrator shall
annually consult with the Chief Counsel for Advocacy of the Office of Advocacy of the Administration to determine best practices for the dissemination of other information relating to cybersecurity matters to small business concerns.
(d) ANNUAL REPORT.—Not later than 90 days
after the date of the enactment of this Act, and
annually thereafter, the Administrator shall
submit to Congress a report that includes the
number and a description of small business concerns that contacted the Administrator during
the year covered by the report with matters relating to the cybersecurity of such concerns.

Small businesses face a growing wave
of cybersecurity threats and may lack
the resources to defend themselves. In
2025 alone, 61 percent of small businesses reported being the victim of at
least one successful cyberattack.
Foreign adversaries use cyberattacks
to steal American intellectual property
and sensitive business information. A
small business with a Federal contract
can be their back door into government
systems.
H.R. 10238 helps close that gap. This
bill directs the SBA to consult directly
with the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency and the National
Institute of Standards and Technology
to deliver cybersecurity information
and resources for small businesses.
It also requires the SBA to provide a
small business with information and

to enter or remain in the Federal market.

RESHORING AMERICAN
MANUFACTURING ACT OF 2026
Mr. WILLIAMS of Texas. Mr. Speaker, I move to suspend the rules and

PO 00000

Frm 00010

E:\CR\FM\A11NO7.007

H11NOPT1

November 11, 2026

pass the bill (H.R. 9718) to require the
Administrator of the Small Business
Administration to maintain a website
for small business concerns relating to
onshoring manufacturing capacity, and
for other purposes, as amended.
The Clerk read the title of the bill.
The text of the bill is as follows:
H.R. 9718
Be it enacted by the Senate and House of Representatives of the United States of America in
Congress assembled,
SECTION 1. SHORT TITLE.

This Act may be cited as the ‘‘Reshoring
American Manufacturing Act of 2026’’ or the
‘‘RAM Act’’.
SEC. 2. INFORMATION AND RESOURCES FOR
SMALL BUSINESS CONCERNS RELATING TO ONSHORING MANUFACTURING CAPACITY.

(a) WEBPAGE REQUIRED.—Not later than 60
days after the date of the enactment of this Act,
the Administrator of the Small Business Administration, acting through the Associate Administrator for the Office of Manufacturing and
Trade of the Administration, shall establish and
maintain a webpage containing a list of resources and information for small business concerns (as defined under section 3 of the Small
Business Act (15 U.S.C. 632)) relating to
onshoring to the United States part or all of the
manufacturing capacity of such concerns.
(b) CONTENTS.—The resources and information included on the webpage required by subsection (a) shall include—
(1) contact information for general inquiries,
relevant offices of the Small Business Administration, and any partner organizations;
(2) a free tool designed to help small business
concerns identify entities that could assist with
onshoring to the United States the manufacturing capacity of such concerns; and
(3) other information, as determined appropriate by the Associate Administrator for the Office of Manufacturing and Trade of the Administration.
(c) UPDATES.—Until the date that is 4 years
after the date of the enactment of this Act, the
Associate Administrator for the Office of Manufacturing and Trade annually shall seek input
from the Chief Counsel for Advocacy of the Office of Advocacy of the Small Business Administration for improvements to the webpage and
updates to the information and resources included on such webpage.
(d) STUDY AND REPORT.—Not later than 2
years after the date on which the webpage is established, the Administrator of the Small Business Administration shall conduct a study and
submit to Congress a report on the effectiveness
of the webpage required under this Act. The Administrator shall submit to Congress an updated
report annually thereafter until the date that is
4 years after the date of the enactment of this
Act.
(e) TERMINATION.—This Act and the requirements of this Act shall terminate 4 years after
the date of the enactment of this Act.

Mr. Speaker, I rise today in strong
support of H.R. 9718, the Reshoring
American Manufacturing Act of 2026,

DUYNE, ELLZEY, and OLSZEWSKI, or
‘‘Big O,’’ as I know him.
For decades, America watched as
manufacturing jobs, production facilities, and critical supply chains moved
overseas. This shift cost American
communities good-paying jobs, made
our small businesses more dependent
on foreign suppliers, and left our economy vulnerable to supply chain disruptions beyond our control.
We cannot afford to continue down
that path.
America’s small businesses create
jobs, support local communities, and
drive innovation. Too often, those
looking to manufacture their products
here at home face unnecessary challenges in identifying domestic suppliers, finding the right resources, and
navigating the process of bringing production back to the United States.
We should be making it easier, not
harder, for American entrepreneurs to
invest, produce, and grow right here at
home.
This is exactly what the legislation
seeks to accomplish.
In May 2025, the SBA launched its
Make Onshoring Great Again portal, a
tool designed to help small businesses
identify American manufacturers and
suppliers.
With a searchable database of more
than 1 million American suppliers, the
portal gives small businesses an easyto-use resource to find domestic partners, strengthen their supply chains,
and keep more economic activity here
in the U.S.
This bill codifies the portal and ensures small businesses have access to
information and resources to bring
manufacturing operations back home,
including connections to relevant
agency offices, partner organizations,
and a free tool to identify entities that
can assist with onshoring manufacturing capacity.
Mr. Speaker, this is a commonsense
approach to supporting American manufacturing and strengthening our small
businesses. I urge my colleagues to
very strongly support H.R. 9718, the Reshoring American Manufacturing Act.

my time.

504 MODERNIZATION AND SMALL
MANUFACTURER ENHANCEMENT
ACT OF 2026
Mr. WILLIAMS of Texas. Mr. Speaker, I move to suspend the rules and
pass the bill (H.R. 10375) to amend the
Small Business Investment Act of 1958
to improve the loan guaranty program,
enhance the ability of small manufacturers to access affordable capital, and
for other purposes, as amended.
The Clerk read the title of the bill.
The text of the bill is as follows:
H.R. 10375
Be it enacted by the Senate and House of Representatives of the United States of America in
Congress assembled,
SECTION 1. SHORT TITLE.

This Act may be cited as the ‘‘504 Modernization and Small Manufacturer Enhancement Act of 2026’’.
SEC. 2. ADDITIONS TO POLICY GOALS FOR THE
DEVELOPMENT
COMPANY
PROGRAM.

Section 501(d)(3) of the Small Business Investment Act of 1958 (15 U.S.C. 695(d)(3)) is
amended—
(1) by redesignating subparagraphs (A)
through (L) as subparagraphs (B) through
(M), respectively;
(2) by inserting before subparagraph (B), as

SEC. 3. IMPROVEMENTS TO 504 LOAN CLOSING
PROCEDURE.

Title V of the Small Business Investment
Act of 1958 (15 U.S.C. 695 et seq.) is amended—
(1) in section 502 (15 U.S.C. 696), by adding
at the end the following:
‘‘(8) CLOSING.—An accredited lender certified company may take any of the following actions to facilitate the closing of a
loan made under this section:
‘‘(A) Reallocate the cost of the project
with respect to which the loan is made in an
amount that is not more than 10 percent of
the overall cost of the project.
‘‘(B) Make typographical corrections to
any name that is applicable to the loan, including the name of any borrower, guarantor, eligible passive company described in
subparagraph (C)(i), and operating company
described in subparagraph (C)(i).
‘‘(C) Add any of the following to receive
proceeds of the loan:
‘‘(i) An eligible passive company, as defined by the Administrator.
‘‘(ii) If an eligible passive company is
added under clause (i), an operating company
with respect to that eligible passive company.
‘‘(D) Make typographical corrections to
the address of any property with respect to
which the loan is made.
‘‘(E) Make typographical corrections to the
name of any interim lender, third-party
lender, or provider of funds described in subclause (I), (II), or (III) of section 502(3)(B)(i).
‘‘(F) Make a guarantor a co-borrower or a
co-borrower a guarantor.
‘‘(G) Add a guarantor that does not have
any ownership interest in the business seeking financing or project being financed under
this section.
‘‘(H) Reduce the amount of debt owed by
the borrower before the closing as a result of
regularly scheduled payments.
‘‘(I) Reduce the cost of the project with respect to which the loan is made.
‘‘(9) ACCREDITED LENDER CERTIFIED COMPANY DEFINED.—In this section, the term ‘accredited lender certified company’ means a
certified development company that the Administration has designated as an accredited
lender under such section 507(b).’’; and
(2) by adding at the end the following:
‘‘SEC. 511. CLOSING AND OVERSIGHT.
‘‘(a) SBA DISTRICT COUNSELS.—Beginning

on the date that is 180 days after the date of

E:\CR\FM\K11NO7.025

H11NOPT1

November 11, 2026

enactment of this section, with respect to
the program established under this title, district counsels of the Administration shall be
subject to the same requirements, and shall
have the same authority and responsibilities,
as in effect with respect to that program on
the day before the date of enactment of this
section, except that—
‘‘(1) the Office of Credit Risk Management
of the Administration shall have the responsibility for all duties relating to conducting
file reviews of loans reviewed and closed by
a designated attorney under this title; and
‘‘(2) district counsels of the Administration
shall not have any responsibility relating to
the review of closing packages with respect
to a loan reviewed and closed by a designated
attorney under this title.
‘‘(b) DESIGNATED ATTORNEYS.—For the purposes of this title, the following definitions
and requirements shall apply with respect to
a designated attorney of a Priority certified
development company:
‘‘(1) The term ‘designated attorney’ means
a certified development company attorney
that the Administrator has approved to close
loans for a Priority certified development
company.
‘‘(2) The term ‘Priority certified development company’ means a certified development company certified to participate on a
permanent basis in the program to provide
financings established under this title and
that the Administrator has approved to participate in an expedited loan and debenture
closing process.
‘‘(3) A designated attorney shall be responsible for certifying documents relating to
the closing of a loan made under this title if
such designated attorney submits to the Administrator evidence that such designated
attorney—
‘‘(A) is licensed and in good standing to
practice law in the State in which such loan
is being closed;
‘‘(B) has professional malpractice insurance coverage; and
‘‘(C) has attended a training course on
closing loans made under this title that is
approved by the Administration.’’.
SEC. 4. SBA MARKETING AND OUTREACH.

Title V of the Small Business Investment
Act of 1958 (15 U.S.C. 695 et seq.), as amended
by this Act, is further amended by adding at
the end the following:
‘‘SEC. 512. SBA MARKETING AND OUTREACH.

‘‘The Administrator shall develop and implement a marketing and outreach plan with
respect to the availability of loans available
under this title and may partner with 1 or
more resource partners to implement such
plan.’’.

SEC. 5. LEASING RULES FOR NEW FACILITIES
AND EXISTING BUILDINGS.
(a) IN GENERAL.—Section 502 of the Small

Business Investment Act of 1958 (15 U.S.C.
696) is amended by striking paragraphs (4)
and (5) and inserting the following:
‘‘(4) NEW FACILITIES.—
‘‘(A) IN GENERAL.—With respect to a
project to construct a new facility, an assisted small business concern may permanently lease not more than 20 percent of the
project to commercial or residential tenants
if such concern—
‘‘(i) permanently occupies and uses not less
than 60 percent of the project;
‘‘(ii) plans to occupy and use an additional
portion of the project that is not permanently leased not later than 3 years after receipt of assistance under this section; and
‘‘(iii) plans to permanently occupy and use
80 percent of the project not later than 10
years after receipt of such assistance.
‘‘(B) SMALL MANUFACTURERS.—With respect
to an assisted small business concern that is
a small manufacturer (as defined in section

VerDate Sep 11 2014

04:03 Nov 12, 2026

H6161

CONGRESSIONAL RECORD — HOUSE

Jkt 079060

501(e)(6)), subparagraph (A)(i) shall apply
with ‘50 percent’ substituted for ‘60 percent’.
‘‘(5) EXISTING BUILDINGS.—With respect to a
project to acquire, renovate, or reconstruct
an existing building, the following shall
apply:
‘‘(A) OCCUPANCY REQUIREMENTS.—The assisted small business concern may permanently lease not more than 50 percent of the
project if the concern permanently occupies
and uses not less than 50 percent of the
project.
‘‘(B) EXCEPTION.—The assisted small business concern may permanently lease more
than 50 percent of the project, but not more
than 66 percent of the project, to commercial
or residential tenants if—
‘‘(i) such concern—
‘‘(I) has occupied and used the existing
building for a consecutive 12-month period
before submitting an application for assistance under this section;
‘‘(II) agrees to permanently use less than
50 percent of the existing building and permanently lease more than 50 percent for a
consecutive 12-month period after receiving
such assistance; and
‘‘(III) affirms that the existing building is
appropriate for current and reasonably anticipated needs; and
‘‘(ii) the development company assisting
such project—
‘‘(I) provides written notice to the Administrator on the date on which the development company closes the loan for such
project; and
‘‘(II) once each year during the first 5 years
of the loan, and once every 2 years for the remainder of the loan—
‘‘(aa) conducts an examination of the assisted small business concern to ensure the
concern is not a real estate development
business; and
‘‘(bb) files with the Administrator an antiinvestor certification signed by the development company and the assisted small business concern.
‘‘(C) LEASE TERM.—Any residential lease
made under this paragraph shall be for a
term of not more than 1 year, and any commercial lease made under this paragraph
shall be for a term of not more than 5
years.’’.
(b) REPORT.—Not later than 5 years after
the date of enactment of this Act, the Administrator of the Small Business Administration shall submit to Congress a report
analyzing the effect of the amendments
made by this section on access to capital for
small business concerns (as defined in section 3 of the Small Business Act (15 U.S.C.
632)).
SEC. 6. CREDIT ELSEWHERE EXEMPTION.

Section 7(a)(1)(A) of the Small Business
Act (15 U.S.C. 636(a)(1)(A)) is amended—
(1) in clause (i), by striking ‘‘The Administrator’’ and inserting ‘‘Except as provided in
clause (iii), the Administrator’’; and
(2) by adding at the end the following:
‘‘(iii) EXEMPTION.—Clause (i) shall not
apply to loans under paragraph (13) of this
subsection, loans or other financings under
the Small Business Investment Act of 1958
(15 U.S.C. 661 et seq.), or any other loan made
by the Administrator to a certified development company.’’.
SEC. 7. APPLICABILITY.

This Act and the amendments made by
this Act shall apply with respect to a loan
made or guaranteed under title V of the
Small Business Investment Act of 1958 (15
U.S.C. 695 et seq.) on or after October 1, 2027.

Mr. WILLIAMS of Texas. Mr. Speaker, I yield myself such time as I may
consume.
Mr. Speaker, before we move to our
final piece of legislation of the day, I
will take a moment to recognize my
friend and colleague, Ms. VELÁZQUEZ,
our ranking member and former chair
of the committee.
The gentlewoman has dedicated
years of service to our committee. We
even have a lot in common. One of
those is that she likes baseball, and she
throws strikes, she tells me.
She has dedicated years of service to
our committee, including her time as
chairwoman, and has been a strong advocate for America’s small businesses.
I have always appreciated her leadership, her commitment to the entrepreneurs whom we represent, and the
bipartisan working relationship we
have developed to make this one of the
best committees we have on the Hill.
We have built that over the years,
and I look forward to continuing that.
Again, I thank the gentlewoman for
her service. It has been a privilege to
serve alongside her, and I personally
thank her for her dedication to the
House Committee on Small Business,
Main Street America, and the advice
that she sometimes gives. I appreciate
that, too.
God bless the gentlewoman.
Mr. Speaker, I rise today in strong
support of H.R. 10375, the 504 Modernization and Small Manufacturer Enhancement Act of 2026, as amended, introduced by myself and Ranking Member VELÁZQUEZ.
The 504 loan program is one of the
SBA’s flagship lending programs, providing small businesses across America
with access to capital. Created 40 years
ago, the 504 loan program has brought
$400 billion in capital to small business
owners and has helped create over 3
million jobs.
That is a remarkable record, but a
program that has served small businesses for four decades must also keep
pace with the challenges that they face
today. Our small businesses are not
standing still, and neither should the
programs designed to support them.
We need to make sure the 504 program reflects modern business needs,
from workforce development to the realities of leasing and expanding commercial space.
These changes will help ensure the
504 program remains relevant and responsive to the needs of today’s small

E:\CR\FM\A11NO7.014

H11NOPT1

H6162

jwatson on DSKGBHNXB4PROD with HOUSE

businesses. From a small manufacturer
trying to develop a workforce to a
mom-and-pop shop trying to revitalize
a historic downtown Main Street,
small businesses of all kinds should
have continued access to the capital
they need to have to grow.
Mr. Speaker, I urge my colleagues to
vote ‘‘yes’’ on H.R. 10375. This bill
keeps the 504 loan program relevant to
current conditions and market spaces
and keeps the American Dream within
reach of everyday Main Street Americans.
Mr. Speaker, I reserve the balance of
my time.
Ms. VELÁZQUEZ. Mr. Speaker, I
yield myself such time as I may consume.
Mr. Speaker, I rise in support of H.R.
10375, the 504 Modernization and Small
Manufacturer Enhancement Act, as
amended.
The 504/CDC program is an SBA loan
guarantee program supporting small
businesses in accessing capital for the
development of major fixed assets, like
buildings and equipment.
This financing has a significant impact on communities across the country. In 2025, the 504/CDC program is estimated to have contributed to the retention and creation of roughly 70,000
new jobs.
The bill will go a long way in boosting CDCs’ ability to support economic
and community development activities
and help small businesses retain and
create jobs.
First, the bill enhances the public
policy goals that CDCs are tasked with
fulfilling, bringing them in line with
our current needs. These goals include
workforce development benchmarks
and prioritizing the expansion of businesses with 10 or fewer employees.
Importantly, for New York, Puerto
Rico, Texas, and hundreds of other
communities across the country, we
have created revitalizing areas impacted by natural disaster as a new
CDC public policy goal. Doing so helps
the 504 program direct loans to disaster
resilience and reinforce the role of
small businesses as the backbone of
local economies during disaster recovery.
b 1230
Further, the bill expedites the 504
loan origination process by enabling
CDCs to carry out aspects of loan closing on their own, while ensuring the
SBA remains involved in the final approval.
A longstanding priority of mine has
been reforming the rules governing
how 504 loans can be used to acquire,
renovate, or reconstruct mixed-use
buildings. There are thousands of
mixed-use buildings across the country, from urban Brooklyn to rural
Texas. These buildings have small businesses on the ground floor—a bodega,
barbershop, or dry cleaner—and small
apartments on the second and third
floors.
Revitalization of our main streets
and urban corridors have stalled for

VerDate Sep 11 2014

November 11, 2026

CONGRESSIONAL RECORD — HOUSE

04:03 Nov 12, 2026

Jkt 079060

decades because we have failed to classify these buildings and develop a government-supported loan program to fit
their needs. By making critical
changes to the leasing requirements,
we can unleash the 504 program’s full
potential to help redevelop these buildings.
Increasing the portion of a building
that a small business may lease out
from 49 to 66 percent allows small businesses to use a 504 loan to properly operate on the street level while leasing
the top floors for residential or commercial use, increasing our commercial
output and housing supply at the same
time.
Finally, my bill provides clarity to
CDCs, lenders, and the SBA that the
‘‘credit elsewhere’’ exemption, which
Congress intended only for the 7(a) program, does not apply to the 504/CDC
program. The 7(a) and 504 programs are
two entirely separate programs, and it
is time SBA and some interested parties treat them as such.
Mr. Speaker, I urge my colleagues to
support my bill, and I reserve the balance of my time.
Mr. WILLIAMS of Texas. Mr. Speaker, we must pass H.R. 10375 to modernize the SBA’s 504 loan program and
ensure small businesses have access to
the capital that they need. I reserve
the balance of my time.
Ms. VELÁZQUEZ. Mr. Speaker, I
yield myself the balance of my time to

make my bill even stronger.

enact this bill into law.
It has been a pleasure to work with
Chairman WILLIAMS on the Small Business Committee, and I wish him the

‘‘yes,’’ and I yield back the balance of
my time.

pace with today’s economy.
From manufacturers expanding their
workforce to Main Street shops revitalizing our communities, this legislation
will keep the American Dream within
reach for small businesses across the
country.
We have the greatest country in the
world. God bless America, and I yield
back the balance of my time.

backed by the entire veteran community and has 342 House cosponsors. It

and divide the community.’’
There is an alternative to the harm
of TCAVA. It is why I stand here today.
The Major Richard Star Act will address a longstanding injustice experienced by thousands of veterans.
The Major Richard Star Act has been
championed for most of the last decade
by my Republican colleague Representative GUS BILIRAKIS, whom I have
nothing but the utmost respect for. He,
along with my good friend Democratic
Representative RAUL RUIZ, has doggedly led the charge to ensure our combat-wounded veterans—nearly 59,000 at
last count—are able to receive the entirety of their earned VA injury and
illness compensation, as well as their
full military retirement.
Under current law, when a servicemember is forced to retire because of

wounds suffered in combat, their military retirement is offset dollar for dollar by the amount of VA compensation
they receive.
That is unconscionable to me, so I
am glad that we are finally, finally
here trying to fix this.
Unfortunately, the path of today’s
action hasn’t been a straight or easy
one. This bill has 342 bipartisan cosponsors this Congress and had over 300 last
Congress, as well.
Despite this yearslong, strong, overwhelming bipartisan support, it will
only receive a vote this week for the

we were successfully able to use the

vote.

should I say perceived cost. For too

concerned about the price tag of this
legislation. This excuse was brought up
repeatedly, even though those same individuals who complain about costs
have no problem sending our soldiers,

trillion dollars. So why then do they
insist on balancing budgets on the
backs of our veterans?

handwringing and disingenuous talk of
debt and deficits, and no more hiding

meaningful action to back them up.

creates a new, lesser class of veterans.

Star Act.

soundly reject the outrageous Republican effort to enact the largest cut to

cuts to veteran benefits in history. It is
called the Take Care of America’s Veterans Act, but it does not take care of
veterans. It takes from them.
It pays for itself by cutting disability
compensation for tinnitus, the most
common service-connected condition
our veterans suffer, and for sleep
apnea.
I am an emergency physician. I know
a bit about tinnitus, and I know a bit
about sleep apnea.
Tinnitus can lead to devastating
hearing dysfunction and hearing loss
that can lead to anxiety, depression,
loss of sleep, matrimonial problems,
and not being able to hear. Many veterans suffer through this day in and
day out, and this bill will take away
the benefits that they have earned in
order to combat this illness.
Sleep apnea is the inability to
breathe and take in oxygen while you
sleep. People wake up with anxiety.
They wake up with palpitations. They
wake up with tachycardia. They wake
up feeling worse than they did the
night before. Their blood pressure rises
20 points. They have 10 years less of life
than somebody who does not have sleep
apnea. It is a real condition with difficulties in their lives. They want to
take away benefits for people and veterans who suffer from sleep apnea.
According to Disabled American Veterans, 1.5 million future veterans would
receive less.
Even its version of the Major Richard
Star Act is watered down. It puts a cap
on what wounded veterans get back.
They still would not receive everything
they have earned. They still will be
shortchanged.

H.R. 1013
Be it enacted by the Senate and House of Representatives of the United States of America in
Congress assembled,
SECTION 1. SHORT TITLE.

This Act may be cited as the ‘‘Retirement
Fairness for Charities and Educational Institutions Act of 2025’’.
SEC. 2. ENHANCEMENT OF 403(b) PLANS.
(a) AMENDMENTS TO THE INVESTMENT COMPANY ACT OF 1940.—Section 3(c)(11) of the In-

vestment Company Act of 1940 (15 U.S.C. 80a–
3(c)(11)) is amended to read as follows:
‘‘(11) Any—
‘‘(A) trust forming part of an employee’s stock
bonus, pension, or profit-sharing plan which
meets the requirements for qualification under
section 401 of the Internal Revenue Code of 1986;
‘‘(B) custodial account meeting the requirements of section 403(b)(7) of such Code;
‘‘(C) governmental plan described in section
3(a)(2)(C) of the Securities Act of 1933 (15 U.S.C.
77c(a)(2)(C));
‘‘(D) collective trust fund maintained by a
bank consisting solely of assets of one or more—
‘‘(i) trusts described in subparagraph (A);
‘‘(ii) governmental plans described in subparagraph (C);
‘‘(iii) church plans, companies, or accounts
that are excluded from the definition of an investment company under paragraph (14) of this
subsection; or
‘‘(iv) plans which meet the requirements of
section 403(b) of the Internal Revenue Code of
1986—
‘‘(I) if—
‘‘(aa) such plan is subject to title I of the Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974
(29 U.S.C. 1001 et seq.);
‘‘(bb) any employer making such plan available agrees to serve as a fiduciary for the plan
with respect to the selection of the plan’s investments among which participants can choose; or
‘‘(cc) such plan is a governmental plan (as defined in section 414(d) of such Code); and
‘‘(II) if the employer, a fiduciary of the plan,
or another person acting on behalf of the employer reviews and approves each investment alternative offered under such plan described
under subclause (I)(cc) prior to the investment
being offered to participants in the plan; or
‘‘(E) separate account the assets of which are
derived solely from—
‘‘(i) contributions under pension or profitsharing plans which meet the requirements of
section 401 of the Internal Revenue Code of 1986
or the requirements for deduction of the employer’s contribution under section 404(a)(2) of such
Code;
‘‘(ii) contributions under governmental plans
in connection with which interests, participations, or securities are exempted from the registration provisions of section 5 of the Securities
Act of 1933 (15 U.S.C. 77e) by section 3(a)(2)(C)
of such Act (15 U.S.C. 77c(a)(2)(C));
‘‘(iii) advances made by an insurance company in connection with the operation of such
separate account; and
‘‘(iv) contributions to a plan described in
clause (iii) or (iv) of subparagraph (D).’’.

(i) if (I) such plan is subject to title I of the Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974

and run the nonprofit organizations
that support the most vulnerable
among us.
Yet, under current Federal securities
law, these public servants and nonprofit workers face an unjust penalty,
a penalty when saving for retirement.
While private-sector workers enjoy
low-cost, high-performing options like
collective investment trusts, or CITs,
in their 401(k)s, our teachers, nurses,
and charity workers are locked out.
Their 403(b) plans are barred from the
same investment options.
The result: higher management fees,
and over a decades-long career, those
hidden fees quietly bleed thousands of
dollars out of a worker’s hard-earned
retirement nest egg.
Madam Speaker, this is simply
wrong.
Congress took a major step forward
when we passed the SECURE 2.0 Act,
updating the tax code to permit CITs
in 403(b) plans. However, tax code
changes alone are not enough if securities regulations remain unaligned.
H.R. 1013 finishes the job by granting
403(b) CITs and separate accounts the
exact same SEC registration exemptions currently enjoyed by 401(k) plans.
Let me be clear: This bill does not
allow direct-to-consumer retail sales.
These options are available exclusively
within institutional, employer-sponsored plans.
Participating plans remain fully governed by ERISA fiduciary standards or
State and local public benefit laws.
Banking regulators continue to supervise CIT structures, while the SEC retains full enforcement authority to
prosecute fraud and misconduct.
This bill is an essential component of
our broader strategy to expand investor access to our capital markets,
something that I, as Capital Markets
Subcommittee chair, have committed
much of my time and tenure here in
Congress to doing, and expanding everyday investor access to our capital
markets.
When the Financial Services Committee advanced my INVEST Act, our
mission was clear: empower entrepreneurs and small businesses, strip
away red tape, and make wealth building accessible to Main Street.
H.R. 1013 extends those exact same
wealth-building options to 15 million
nonprofit workers. This legislation
passed the Financial Services Committee with sweeping bipartisan support, and it passed the House floor as
part of the INVEST Act with 302 votes,
including every House Republican and
87 House Democrats, and with support
from 81 outside organizations.
This bill, in particular, has the backing of several organizations focused on
helping Americans achieve a secure retirement, including the Insured Retirement Institute, the SPARK Institute,
and the American Council of Life Insurers.

strengthening our communities should

Mr. LYNCH. Madam Speaker, I yield
myself such time as I may consume.
Madam Speaker, just a week ago, in
a historic election, the American people sent Congress a clear message:
Bring down costs, end the forever wars,
and stop the corruption that is lining
the pockets of the White House insiders, billionaires, and corporate executives.
Families are struggling to pay for
rent and groceries, while the President’s reckless and illegal tariffs push
their prices even higher. They are paying outrageous prices at the gas pump
and on their utility bills while billionaires and the Trump family get richer
and richer.
After hearing that resounding message from voters, what is the first bill
that Republicans bring up? It is not a
bill to lower costs, to end the war, to
rein in AI, or to clean up corruption. It
is a giveaway to banks, insurance companies, Wall Street investment firms,
and unregulated retirement consultants.
Who pays for that giveaway? Teachers, nurses, and public servants, with
their retirement savings, pay for it.
H.R. 1013 is widely opposed by the
Americans it would most affect. The
same people whose savings are at stake
have told us where they stand. Teachers, nurses, public servants, and workers across the country do not want
their retirement plans deregulated at
the behest of Wall Street.
That is why this bill is opposed by
groups such as: AARP; AFL–CIO;
American Federation of Teachers; National Education Association; National
Nurses United; Americans for Financial Reform; Service Employees International
Union;
Communications
Workers of America; American Federation of State, County, and Municipal
Employees; the UAW; North American
Securities Administrators Association;
Public Citizen; and the Consumer Federation of America.
This bill would strip safeguards from
403(b) plans, which are how teachers,
nurses, and the staff of hospitals and
charities save for retirement. These
plans hold $1.6 trillion. This is not a
small change.
Today, the mutual funds and variable
annuities sold to these plans must be
registered with the Securities and Exchange Commission. Registration gives
a plan standard information on fees

PO 00000

Frm 00019

Fmt 4634

Sfmt 0634

and risks so it can compare its options
on behalf of the workers who have put
their hard-earned money into it.
H.R. 1013 would let banks and insurance companies sell these plans two
kinds of products without registering
them with the SEC: collective investment trusts and insurance company
separate accounts.
A school district choosing investments for its teachers’ retirement plan
would no longer get the standardized
detailed information it gets today.
That is the information it needs to
make the best investment decision possible. Unlike a mutual fund, a collective investment trust has no independent board approving its fees.
H.R. 1013, however, would let banks
and insurance companies sell two kinds
of financial products without registering them with the Securities and
Exchange Commission. Republicans
call this parity with 401(k) plans. It is
not.
Nearly every 401(k) plan is covered by
the safeguards of the Employee Retirement Income Security Act, or ERISA,
which holds the people who run a plan
to a fiduciary standard and requires
fees to be disclosed to every participant. However, the vast majority of
403(b) plans are not covered by ERISA
at all.
This bill lets the employer, who is
often busy with their day-to-day job,
hand the job of approving investments
to ‘‘another person acting on behalf of
the employer.’’ This bill does not require that that person be a fiduciary,
that is to act in the best interests of
the plan and to be registered with a
Federal agency or even to be independent of the companies whose products they sell.
This bill also specifically exempts
these products from U.S. securities
laws, so a firm that sells only these
products would not have to register
with the SEC as a broker-dealer, and
the SEC’s best interest rule, which ensures that a financial intermediary is
acting in the best interest of the retirement plan. That would not apply. As a
result, unregulated retirement consultants will end up nickel-and-diming our
teachers’ retirement options and our
nurses’ retirement options.
Teachers have seen what hidden conflicts cost. For example, in 2020, a subsidiary of a major annuity company
agreed to pay about $40 million to settle SEC charges because the SEC found
that it failed to disclose to Florida
teachers that its parent company had
paid an outside group for 13 years to
promote it. That case depended on the
SEC’s oversight of the firms that sell
to teachers. This bill removes the SEC
from protecting those very same teachers.
Collective investment trusts can also
take risks that mutual funds cannot.
Mutual funds face limits on the illiquid
assets and on borrowing. Federal banking rules set no such limits for collective trusts. Some of these trusts are
run by State-chartered trust companies with no Federal oversight at all.

Meanwhile, the Trump administration is moving to put risky private equity and private credit investments
into workers’ retirement plans. Collective investment trusts are a clear way

is not that approach. Democrats have
offered a better path.

benefit all investors by requiring them

Either amendment would change this

Mr. LUCAS. Madam Speaker, I rise
today in strong support of my bill, H.R.
1013, the Retirement Fairness for Charities and Educational Institutions Act.
This bipartisan legislation will allow
our communities’ public servants, such
as teachers, nurses, doctors, university
professionals, and employees of nonprofit organizations, to have the same
financial tools available as the private
sector when planning for their retirement.
For many of these public servants,
their retirement savings are through
403(b) plans. However, those public
servants’ 403(b) plans are unable to invest in the same cost-effective investment products that are available to
other plans like 401(k) plans.
Under current law, 403(b) plans generally cannot access the same collec-

VerDate Sep 11 2014

November 11, 2026

CONGRESSIONAL RECORD — HOUSE

04:03 Nov 12, 2026

Jkt 079060

tive investment trusts and insurance
company separate accounts available
to 401(k) plans.
Collective investment trusts allow
investment plans to pool their investments, providing access to institutional investment strategies at lower
costs than many traditional mutual
funds.
Similarly, insurance company separate accounts provide another avenue
for retirement plans to access professionally managed investments, generally with lower administrative expenses. The advantage is that these
plans can be less expensive to operate,
leaving more of the investment return
to the worker’s account.
b 1350
Over the course of someone’s career,
even small reductions in investment
fees can translate into thousands of additional dollars in retirement savings.
Consider two Americans saving for retirement. One works for a private company and saves through a 401(k). The
other works for a public school or a
nonprofit and saves through a 403(b).
Both are putting away their hardearned money for retirement, but because of outdated restrictions, the
worker with a 403(b) does not have access to the same lower-cost investment
options.
Now, this may seem like a small difference, but consider a public servant
saving $10,000 a year over a 40-year career. Assuming a 6-percent annual return, paying just 5 basis points more in
annual investment fees could cost that
worker more than $19,000 in retirement
savings. That is a significant amount
of money for someone who has spent
their career serving their community.
Our public servants deserve better.
My legislation would give those workers access to the same investment options available to millions of other
Americans. This bill would not only
provide a level playing field for workers, regardless of the type of retirement plan their employer sponsors, but
would also allow our nonprofit institutions like hospitals, public schools, and
charities, to compete with the private
sector for talent through their retirement plan offers.
The effort to finally provide parity in
retirement options for our public servants is a bipartisan one spanning several Congresses.
I am grateful to my colleagues on
both sides of the aisle who have worked
hard to ensure that those who serve
our communities have equal access to
save for retirement.
In the 117th Congress, the Ways and
Means Committee unanimously passed
this measure under former Chairman
NEAL’s leadership. While only the tax
code was ultimately fixed for 403(b)
plans that year, it was a much-needed
first step in correcting this disparity.
The vote was nearly unanimous, 414–5.
I introduced this bill in the 118th
Congress to finish the work that the
Ways and Means Committee began and

to correct the underlying securities
laws. In 2023, the Financial Services

This Congress’ Financial Services

this legislation with 43 yeas. Just last

Congresses, this legislation has received bipartisan support from both
committees and on the House floor. It
is time we finish the work and ensure
that teachers, nonprofit employees,

to the same retirement investment options as private-sector workers.
Madam Speaker, I urge my colleagues to support this bill so that

Mr. LYNCH. Madam Speaker, I yield

Madam Speaker, I have the greatest
respect for the gentleman from Oklahoma, but I do need to take exception
to the claim that collective investment
trusts cost less than mutual funds on
average and that allowing them would
make it more affordable for teachers.
Collective investment trusts might
have a lower price, but that is only because the safeguards that apply to
those products have been stripped from
them. They are gone. Products cost
less, Madam Speaker, when you strip
out the rules that protect the people
who buy those products.
Think
about
our
automobiles.
Madam Speaker, you could sell a car
more cheaply if you stripped out the
seatbelts and the airbags. Adding the
FDA to the process of drug approval
probably makes it more expensive to
produce those drugs. Those drugs
might get cheaper if they didn’t have
to go through the FDA, but I don’t
think anyone really believes that that
is how to make medicine more affordable, and it isn’t how to make retirement affordable.
Proponents of this bill argue that
collective investment trusts cost less
than mutual funds on average, but on
average isn’t what a teacher pays. Collective investment trust fees are set by
share class and negotiated plan by
plan, so the biggest discount goes to
the biggest plans. A small school district or a small community hospital insuring their nurses has the least bargaining power and would end up in the
most expensive plan.
The fact is that as long as collective
investment trusts do not disclose the
same information as mutual funds, we
don’t really know whether they deliver
on the promise to offer an inexpensive
and equally safe investment option for
teachers and nurses. If collective investment trusts are cheaper, then let

E:\CR\FM\K11NO7.039

H11NOPT1

jwatson on DSKGBHNXB4PROD with HOUSE

November 11, 2026

them prove it, and that is by a side-byside fee comparison with a similar mutual fund and the costs that those represent.
If they are as cheap as the proponents say, publishing their fees
would only help them sell those products. The bill before us doesn’t require
these disclosures, doesn’t require those
listings, and doesn’t require that production. Ranking Member WATERS
tried getting that level of disclosure in
this bill, but she was unsuccessful.
Fees aren’t the only cost. The assets
held by collective investment trusts
can also be risky and opaque. A collective investment trust has no independent board, and Federal banking
rules put no limit on how much it can
hold in hard-to-sell assets or how much
it can borrow. A lower fee is no bargain
if the fund is loaded with private assets
that are hard to value, hard to sell, and
nobody knows how these funds will perform when the going gets rough in the
financial markets.
Teachers can already get low fees.
Index mutual funds are available for a
few hundredths of 1 percent a year.
Teachers need better plans and clear
information, not fewer protections.
Now, we are not against all collective
investment trusts. Democrats propose
letting 403(b) plans use them but with
public disclosure of fees and holdings
and disclosure of what the middlemen
are being paid. No one is barred. They
just have to disclose that information.
That gets teachers the savings without
the hidden risks, but Republicans
would not include that provision in
this bill, and our amendments were not
allowed.

RECOGNIZING ALL WHO HAVE
ANSWERED THE CALL
(Mr. MESSMER asked and was given
permission to address the House for 1
minute and to revise and extend his remarks.)
Mr. MESSMER. Mr. Speaker, on this
day set aside to honor America’s veterans, I rise to recognize the brave men
and women who have worn the uniform
of the United States Armed Forces.
During difficult times of war, generations of Hoosiers from Indiana’s Eighth
District have answered the call and left
their families, their communities, and
the comforts of home to defend our
country and protect the freedoms we
hold dear.
Our responsibility to the needs of
those great heroes does not end when
their Active-Duty service is over. I join
my colleagues in making sure that
every United States veteran will always
receive
the
benefits,
the
healthcare, and all the support they
have earned and deserve.
We honor and thank every Hoosier
and every American who has served our
grateful Nation, today and every day,
for their courageous sacrifice. May God
bless our beloved veterans and their
families, and may God bless the United
States of America.

HONORING THE MEMORY OF
WALLACE D. MALONE, JR.
(Mr. PALMER asked and was given
permission to address the House for 1
minute and to revise and extend his remarks.)
Mr. PALMER. Mr. Speaker, I rise
today to honor the memory of my
friend, Wallace D. Malone, Jr.
Malone was born August 3, 1936, in
Dothan, the son of Wallace D. Malone,
Sr., and Alice Mae Dee Malone.
Wallace earned his bachelor’s degree
in business in 1957 from the University
of Alabama. He earned an MBA degree
in 1959 from the Wharton School of
Business at the University of Pennsylvania.
I first met Wallace in the early 1990s
when he was chairman and CEO of
SouthTrust Bank, which he founded.
Wallace was a legendary figure in
banking, building SouthTrust into the
largest financial organization in Alabama and one of America’s Fortune 500
companies.
His family entered banking over 100
years ago in one office in the heart of
Dothan, Alabama.
Wallace was inducted into the Alabama Academy of Honor in 1986. In
1996, The Birmingham News named
Wallace CEO of the Year, and in 2001,
Wallace was inducted into the Alabama
Business Hall of Fame.
Malone invested his time and treasure in his community and State. He
served on numerous boards supporting
healthcare, business, and numerous
nonprofits, as well as serving on the
Federal Reserve Branch in Birmingham. He also supported education
through the Malone Family Foundation, and he was one of the early and
longtime supporters of the Alabama
Family Alliance, later the Alabama
Policy Institute, the think tank that I
had the honor of leading.
Wallace was, first and foremost, a
man of faith and a family man. He and
his wife, Ocllo, were married 48 years,
parents to 6 children, 17 grandchildren,
and 9 great-grandchildren.
Wallace was a dear friend and counselor to me and to so many others. He
was always generous in sharing his insights and wisdom, and I always en-

PO 00000

Frm 00024

Fmt 4634

Sfmt 0634

joyed my time with him. He was truly
one of a kind.

NEW GOVERNOR IN DEEP RED
TEXAS
(Mr. GREEN of Texas asked and was
given permission to address the House
for 1 minute and to revise and extend
his remarks.)
Mr. GREEN of Texas. Mr. Speaker,
deep in the heart of red Texas, a miracle has been given birth, and that miracle has caused a female to be selected
to be the next speaker of the Texas
House. It is significant, Mr. Speaker,
because when I was there to hear the
announcement, I was in the room
where they have the pictures of all of
the prior speakers of the Texas House,
and they have something in common:
They were all men, and they were all
White.
Mr. Speaker, the next speaker of the
Texas House of Representatives will be
a woman, and she will be a Black
woman.
God bless the United States of America. Deep in the heart of Texas, the
Governor will be a female, the Lieutenant Governor will be a female, and the
speaker of the house will be a female.

REMEMBERING JUDGE STANLEY
LEVINE
(Mr. STUTZMAN asked and was

his remarks.)
Mr. STUTZMAN. Mr. Speaker, I rise
today to honor the life and legacy of
Judge Stanley Levine of Fort Wayne,
Indiana, who passed away last month
at the age of 88.
Judge Levine is remembered in many
ways for his service to his community
and his country. Beginning in 1998, he
served 20 years on the Allen County
Superior Court where he was highly respected for his integrity and his quick
wit. He also served his country in the
Air National Guard at the 122nd Fighter Wing in Fort Wayne, Indiana.
Outside of these accomplishments,
Judge Levine gave back to his community through his work with Heartland
Sings, where he shared his love of
music and singing.
Judge Levine’s life is an example to
all Hoosiers on how to truly serve others in everything that we do.
My prayers go out to Judge Levine’s
family. May he rest in peace.

Troy has spent decades in government relations, most recently at the

Mr. MOORE of Utah. Mr. Speaker, on
Veterans Day, we salute the brave men
and women who have worn the uniform
of the United States of America.

Mr. HARRIS of North Carolina. Mr.
Speaker, I rise to honor the more than
38,000 veterans who call North Carolina’s Eighth District home and the
millions of brave men and women
across our great Nation who have
proudly worn the uniform of the United
States Armed Forces.
From the battlefields of the Revolutionary War to the conflicts of today,
generations of Americans have answered the call to serve, putting their
lives on the line for the freedoms that
we all hold dear.
As a son of a World War II veteran, I
know that the strength of our Nation is

VerDate Sep 11 2014

04:03 Nov 12, 2026

H6173

CONGRESSIONAL RECORD — HOUSE

Jkt 079060

rooted in the courage and character of
those who have defended it. Our veterans come from every walk of life.
Whether serving on the front lines in
times of war or standing ready during
times of peace, each one of them has
shared a commitment to something
greater than themselves.
That commitment has often come at
a tremendous personal cost. Some
made the ultimate sacrifice and never
returned home. Others returned carrying wounds, both seen and unseen.
For countless families, military service meant years apart, resulting in
missed birthdays, holidays, and precious moments that can never be replaced.
Their service does not end when they
take off the uniform. Our veterans continue making a difference as teachers,
first responders, businessowners, and
community leaders.
Let us remember that Veterans Day
is not simply a day of recognition. It is
a reminder that the freedoms we enjoy
were not freely given and that our
gratitude must extend far beyond a single day.
We owe our veterans more than
words of thanks. We owe them the respect, care, and benefits they have
earned through their service to our
country.

our Nation, let me say: Thank you.

Thank you for the sacrifices you have
made. Thank you for the example that
you have set for generations of Americans.

Mr. MOORE of Utah. Mr. Speaker, I

Mr. MEUSER. Mr. Speaker, I rise
today to celebrate and acknowledge
Veterans Day, a time all Americans
should reflect on the courage and valor
of so many men and women who have
put on the uniform to defend our Nation.
‘‘Thank you for your service’’ should
be said often every day, and especially
today, to these American heroes.
Mr. Speaker, we owe our veterans
more than words of gratitude. We must
deliver the benefits and support they
need after their service in bills like my
Veterans Housing Opportunity Act,
legislation that ensures veterans can
access high-quality benefits they have
earned through their service, as well as
the Major Richard Star Act, which is
very important for veterans throughout our Nation and certainly in my district.
One veteran I would like to recognize
today is my friend, retired Lackawanna County judge and Vietnam vet-

PO 00000

Frm 00025

Fmt 4634

Sfmt 0634

eran Thomas Munley. This American
hero showed courage on the battlefield
and here at home.
Incidentally, he was recognized just
this last week as the 2026 Tribute to
Courage honoree at the Spirit of Hope
Celebration, which is chaired by my
friend, the one-and-only Leo Vergnetti.
It is an annual event to raise money
and awareness for the Northeast Regional Cancer Institute, which has
helped thousands of low-income individuals access direct cancer-related
healthcare services.
As a Vietnam veteran and cancer survivor, Tom Munley has made it his
mission to do everything in his power
to help those battling cancer and support his fellow veterans.
Through his ‘‘Veterans Views’’ segment on WBRE News in Pennsylvania,
Tom highlights local heroes and brings
attention to the issues most pressing
to veterans across America. They have
had me on a few times, as well.
As Tom makes clear on his program,
fighting for those who have given so
much to defend our country is a tremendous responsibility, and we must
prioritize efforts that live up to our
commitment to our heroes.
Earlier this year, I joined Tom on
‘‘Veterans Views’’ to discuss my bill,
the Veterans Housing Opportunity Act,
legislation that would increase the
availability of housing assistance to
homeless veterans by redirecting unused veterans housing vouchers to
communities where the need is greatest.

of right ought to be free and independent States . . . ’’ changed the

As our Founders put pen to paper, a

very unique idea, an idea that all men
are created equal and endowed by their
