I am a proud son of a World War II
veteran, Clarence Burgess Owens. Born
100 years ago, my dad was raised in the
era of the Great Depression and nationwide segregation. He and his two brothers proudly volunteered to serve in
World War II.
My dad, due to his rifle skills, was
part of our nation’s first integrated reconnaissance team. He was trained in
Alabama at Fort McClellan’s Infantry
Replacement Training Center as part
of an Army experiment, an experiment
of leadership integration in which
Black and White officers were training
Black enlisted soldiers.
I was blessed to have a dad who always was my hero. He was exceptional
but not an exception for the men of
that generation. He knew how to
dream big, work hard, compete for respect, and when necessary, run toward
his fears, as did millions of young
American soldiers during past wars.
Last year, I ran across notes from his
journal that summarized his feelings as
a 20-year-old upon returning home
from the Philippines and Japan. He
wrote: ‘‘I have fulfilled my dream to
serve my country.’’
After returning home from war, dad
earned his Ph.D. at Ohio State in
agronomy, taught 40 years at FAMU,
was a successful entrepreneur and father of five. He joined an aggregate of
his fellow veterans who built the greatest diverse middle class in our Nation’s
history. They simultaneously tore
down the walls of our country’s racial
divide.
This is what defines our history as a
nation: brave, patriotic Americans who
answer the call, serve our Nation in
uniform, and then once that chapter is
over, continue to find ways to continue
to serve our country. It is a proud history of dedication and sacrifice. It is
the greatest legacy we can give to
those who come after us.
Our Founders sought to create a
more perfect Union. Millions of Americans now, and millions in the future,
will benefit from the faith-filled veterans who sacrifice for our country
through their blood, sweat, and tears.

across this Nation, to those who
stormed the beaches, patrolled jungles
and deserts, and stood watch on quiet

One such occasion was June 6, 1944,
when American and Allied Forces
stormed the beaches of Normandy in
what would become one of the most
consequential military operations in
world history. Operation Overlord
ended up being the turning point in
World War II, paving the way for the
liberation of France and eventually the
defeat of Nazi Germany.
We know how impactful that day
was, but then, in the moments before
they hit the shore, those soldiers had
no idea what that day had in store.
They did not know whether they would
survive, whether their mission would
succeed, or whether they would ever
see their families again. But when the
landing craft reached the beach and the
ramps came down, those men ran headlong into hell.
b 1750
Over 4,000 allied troops were killed
that day, including over 2,500 Americans.
As I have thought of those losses, I
couldn’t help but wonder—who was the
first that morning? Did he get off the
landing craft? Did his foot ever touch
water or sand? When you consider this,
you have to be in awe of the courage of
that young American soldier. But not
just him, the guy behind him who kept
going, the guy behind that guy, and the
guys behind them that kept charging
into that firestorm of lead and fire.
Each of those men faced a choice in a
moment of unimaginable danger. They
could not know the outcome of the battle, but they understood the importance of their mission and what was at
stake.
Those young men understood what it
meant to do what is required. They
carried on with courage and determination. Without their commitment, the
world we know today would look drastically different. As I said, on that day,
over 2,500 Americans laid their lives on
the altar of freedom never knowing
what they accomplished or experiencing the profound gratitude of those
for whom they had died.
As this year marks America’s 250th
anniversary, we are reminded that our
Nation and our freedoms would not
have endured without the courage and
sacrifice of those who have worn the
uniform. Our veterans have been willing to stand in harm’s way so that future generations might live in freedom.
That is a legacy we must never take
for granted and a sacrifice we must
never forget.
There are over 400,000 veterans living
in Alabama. As of 2025, 394 World War
II veterans and among those, one is
over 107 years old.
Mr. Speaker, I thank every veteran
for their service and unwavering commitment to our country and their willingness to do what is required.

HONORING THE LIFE OF COACH RON RIDDLE

Mr. BEAN of Florida. Mr. Speaker, I
rise to honor the life and legacy of
Coach Ron Riddle, a beloved educator,
mentor, and coach at Clay High School
in Green Cove Springs, Florida.
There are teachers who educate,
coaches who inspire, and community
leaders who make a difference. Every
once in a while someone comes along
and does all three. For generations of
Clay High Blue Devils, that person was
Coach Ron Riddle.
Coach Riddle was more than a coach.
He was a mentor and a role model. For
countless young people, he was someone who believed in them before they
believed in themselves.
He was a trailblazer. As one of the
first Black teachers at Clay High
School during integration, Coach Riddle helped break down barriers and
open doors for others. For 37 years, he
dedicated himself to teaching and
coaching, instilling lessons that went
far beyond athletics, discipline, perseverance, self-respect, and the importance of looking out for one another.
My wife, Abby, was fortunate enough
to know Coach Riddle. Growing up in
Green Cove Springs, she saw firsthand
the tremendous influence he had on her
and so many of her classmates. His encouragement stayed with them long
after their days at Clay High School.
One of those former players, Rick
Wheeler, recently shared a beautiful
tribute. Rick said Coach Riddle
coached them for 10 years, from Pop
Warner through High School. He said
something that captured Coach’s legacy perfectly: ‘‘If you were privileged
to be coached by him, you were a better player, but more importantly, you
were a better person.’’ That is what
made Coach Riddle so special.
Coach Riddle was part of Clay High
School’s championship football tradition. After retiring, he helped tirelessly to bring a new track to Clay
High. That track was fittingly named
in his honor. His legacy isn’t about a
championship or track, it is the generations of young people whose lives he
changed.

left it all on the field—now enjoy your

It is fitting that on the 11th of November, a day we call Veterans Day—a
tradition that began in the wake of
World War I, the 11th hour of the 11th
day of the 11th month—we use this day
to remember the sacrifices of the people who sacrificed for us.
It is with a mixture of reverence for
their service, but also, because of the
events today, I am angry. I am sad. I
am confused by what I feel is the unjust and unfair way that the majority
has conducted themselves with regard
to how this Chamber works.
I am trying to contain and make sure
that my anger does not explode to the
extent that I will say things I will regret.
But my deepest regret is that on this

would have seen, but for the distractions, the decoys that the majority
used to distract and to put forward and
move forward through a specious voice

Veterans Act, which in reality is an
ironic title.
There is nothing really about this act
that takes care of veterans. It is the
largest cut to veterans’ benefits in the
history of the United States.
The way that the majority contrived
to push this bill through was to use decoys in a decoy way, the floor staff, to
negotiate technical changes to the bill
that we were supposed to agree to on
unanimous consent, and while this was
happening, they very quietly put forward a vote on TCAVA.

right for a recorded vote. There was no
opportunity later for the majority to
grant that vote. As a result, there has
been no accountability for the Members of this Chamber to take responsibility for what they would have voted
on on TCAVA.

good for veterans, I don’t understand
why there would have been an eagerness to be recorded as an ‘‘aye’’ vote.

Their intention was not to have an
open vote, a roll call vote. Their intention was to sneak this through.

shallow and hollow victory because it
will go nowhere in the Senate. Why?
Because veterans service organizations

America’s Veterans Act, TCAVA. They
have made clear that the largest cut to
veterans’ benefits in history is simply
not acceptable.

through this bill in the way that they
did on today, of all days.

the explosions and the blasts.

Ms. KAPTUR. Mr. Speaker, I rise to
honor and recognize our veterans on
this Veterans Day 2026. Let us salute
and thank all veterans for their noble
service to our Nation. I thank their
loved ones for their sacrifice.
Just in my region of the United
States, I represent over 60,000 veterans
in northwestern Ohio, and I consider it
the honor of a lifetime to serve veterans and military families across our
region of northwestern Ohio, but also
wherever they might be stationed in
our Nation and across the world. America appreciates them. We see them. We
love them.
I come from a long line of beloved
uncles, cousins, and family members
who worked in our war industry. All of
us were steeped in patriotism and military service. They instilled in me the
need to serve in my own way and give
back to our Nation that afforded my
family freedom and opportunity.
I have made support for America’s
veterans a major focus of my career in
Congress. I want to put this on the
record tonight because, for some of the
younger Members coming in here from
all over the Nation, if they work at it
long enough, they can really do something here.
The first committee assignment I received was to serve on the Veterans’
Affairs Committee. I continue to serve
our veterans today as a senior member
of the Appropriations Subcommittee

VerDate Sep 11 2014

05:32 Nov 12, 2026

Jkt 079060

on Defense. I ended up, in that early
service on the Veterans’ Affairs Committee, as the only woman. I still remember that.
Two of the most noble fights I have
led in Congress were the long legislative battles—first, to build a World
War II Memorial here in our Nation’s
Capital. It took us 16 years. Now, 100
million Americans and other visitors
have gone to that very sacred place.
It took 16 years to build the Veterans’ Glass City Skyway Bridge across
the largest river that flows into the
Great Lakes, back home in Toledo,
Ohio. Millions of vehicles have traversed that bridge now. The construction and manufacture of all the parts
that went into that took an additional
16 years and probably about $400 million over several cycles of Congress.
Along the way, I helped lead a community effort to save the 180th Fighter
Wing when it was threatened by base
closure. That wing has grown to be 1 of
the 10 most strategically important
wings in our country. We are proud of
them and their achievements right in
the heart of northern Ohio.
We advocate every single day for the
brave men and women who serve our
Nation to defend the cause of liberty.
One of our greatest achievements has
been the construction of the veterans
outpatient clinic in Toledo, Ohio.
I also helped secure funding to open
Defiance County’s first VA-based outpatient clinic to serve our veterans on
the western side of the State, and I
continue to advocate for the Sandusky
veterans home, which has served thousands of veterans in their elderly years.
I continue to push for dual affiliation
between our University of Toledo Medical Center and the Department of Veterans Affairs hospital system so that
we can actually have the affiliation inside the State of Ohio rather than
being one of the few States in the
Union where you have to cross borders
to get to a veterans hospital.
I was able to authorize and secure
funding for the largest expansion of VA
care in American history through the
passage of the Sergeant First Class
Heath Robinson Honoring our Promise
to Address Comprehensive Toxics Act
of 2022, named after an Ohioan with
family roots in Ohio’s Ninth District.
Burn pits and other toxic hazards
have grievously wounded servicemembers from Vietnam, Iraq, and Afghanistan. Named in honor of Sergeant First
Class Heath Robinson of Ohio, the
PACT Act has expanded healthcare to
3.5 million veterans across our Nation.
All Ohioans are proud of that achievement, and we have given it to the Nation and to those courageous veterans.
I will continue to fight for this funding to stay accessible to veterans who
served in Vietnam, the Gulf war, and
the Middle East conflicts of Iraq and
Afghanistan. They have served us, and
now the Heath Robinson act will serve
their health, well-being, and, believe
me, that of their caring families.
When I returned to Washington this
week, I was reminded of the excellent

by the majority earlier today and I
hope we can pick up the broken pieces
tomorrow.
After announcing a recorded vote on
a benefits bill, which would only support some veterans by robbing others
and taking money from other veteran
programs, the majority began a conversation with the Democrats to discuss possible bipartisan changes. Then
this majority used the absence of
Democrats on the House floor to callup the bill by voice vote and pass it.
It was one of those legislative tricks
that really isn’t worthy of occurring on
this floor. Shady actions like this don’t
happen without intention. This was a
setup to pass partisan legislation over
a stronger piece of bipartisan legislation. I think that is going to end soon,
but the Senate should reject this trickery, and we should have passed the
Major Richard Star Act as a clean
stand-alone bill.
On Veterans Day of all days, our veterans deserve better than that trickery. They deserve to be represented by
people who share their morals and,
frankly, their honor. I hope that in the
new Congress, we can truly treat them
with the honor and respect they have
earned and deserve.
Mr. Speaker, I yield back the balance
of my time on this very memorable day
of this year of 2026.

Thomas Massie, move to discharge the

purposes, in support of which motion
the undersigned Members of the House
of Representatives affix their signatures, to wit:

Senate
The Senate met at 12 noon and was
called to order by the President pro
tempore (Mr. GRASSLEY).
f

PRAYER
The Chaplain, Dr. Barry C. Black, offered the following prayer:
Let us pray.
Almighty God, who surrounds us
with angels who excel in strength. We
thank You for this day set aside to
honor those who have served our Nation in the U.S. Armed Forces.
We remember with gratitude those
who served in times of war and peace,
those who stood firm with courage
when challenges and sacrifices were required, those who helped to protect our
freedom.
Bless our veterans. Heal those who
carry physical and emotional wounds.
Comfort
grieving
families.
And
strengthen those who continue to bear
the burdens of military service.
May our appreciation be reflected not
merely in our words but in our willingness to serve others, defend what is
right, and become instruments of Your
peace.
Surround our veterans and their families with Your grace and reward their
sacrifices with the assurance that they
are remembered and appreciated.
We pray in the Name of the Prince of
Peace. Amen.
f

PLEDGE OF ALLEGIANCE
The President pro tempore led the
Pledge of Allegiance, as follows:
I pledge allegiance to the Flag of the
United States of America, and to the Republic for which it stands, one nation under God,
indivisible, with liberty and justice for all.

The PRESIDING OFFICER. The Senator from Iowa.
VETERANS DAY

Mr. GRASSLEY. Mr. President,
today is Veterans Day. We were reminded of that by the prayer of the
Senate chaplain. But I hope we don’t
have to be reminded of it because this
is a special day of thanks and remembrance for those who served.
Originally, what we call Veterans
Day was called Armistice Day at the

end of World War I. On the 11th hour of
the 11th day of the 11th month of 1918,
it was all quiet on the Western Front.
And I believe there was a movie named
that way.
Anyway, from my hometown of New
Hartford, IA, where I have lived my entire life—700 residents—they are reminded of this sacrifice that we honor
today. The two names are Geyer and
Bricker, two New Hartford residents
who lost their lives in World War I. The
American Legion post is named after
them.
Veterans Day, as I said before, is a
time of remembrance for the sacrifices
of brave men and women who have
made that sacrifice to preserve freedom and liberty here at home and to
broadcast liberty and freedom around
the world.
Veterans sacrifice for our country.
So then it is our responsibility to take
care of them when they come home.
Now, everybody has that responsibility, but for those of us in public
life—through lawmaking and oversight
and case work, I work to ensure Iowa
veterans have the resources they need,
and I work to hold the Federal Government accountable to the promises it
has made to those who served in uniform.
Just one of the projects that I have
for veterans is my office hosting a Veterans History Project event around
Veterans Day to collect stories from
Iowa veterans and submit those stories
to the Library of Congress to honor
their service for generations to come
but also for any American that wants
to know what it is to serve in the military and particularly in wartime. They
can go and understand those same stories that I understand when I receive
them from the veterans.
So this past Friday, my office’s Veteran History Project was held at the
American Legion Post 403 in Van
Meter, IA. So far, including last week,
with our several years of interviews, I
have submitted over 100 stories from

Iowa veterans to the Library of Congress.
So I say to all veterans: Thank you
for your service.
I yield the floor.

CONGRESSIONAL RECORD — SENATE

Mr. THUNE. Mr. President, yesterday the Republican conference held its
first meeting with our new Members
for the 120th Congress, and we had a robust discussion about our conference’s
future and how we can best serve the
American people for the next 2 years.
We also held our leadership elections
for the coming Congress. I am grateful
to my colleagues for again placing
their trust in me to be the Republican
leader.
And I am looking forward to working
with the other Members of our leadership team and with all of our colleagues, both old and new, over the
next 2 years.
In my first speech from this desk
nearly 2 years ago, one of the things I
talked about was restoring the Senate
as a place of discussion and deliberation. And over these last 2 years, I have
striven for that ideal, and I am proud
of the legislative record that we have
built. The Working Families Tax Cuts,
which delivered permanent tax relief to
hard-working Americans, the 21st Century ROAD to Housing Act, a bipartisan effort to make home ownership
more affordable.
Republicans and Democrats came together to craft the first Federal legislation dealing with digital assets. We
passed the Laken Riley Act, the Protect College Sports Act, and we demonstrated what is possible when both
parties come together for a more open
appropriations process. On nominations, we faced historic obstruction
from Democrats, and despite that, we
got President Trump’s Cabinet in place
at the fastest pace in over two decades.
We have confirmed 605 civilian nominees and put 56 article III judges on the
Federal bench, and at the fastest pace
since the Reagan administration. And
we are not done.
We could not have confirmed all of
these individuals or crafted and passed
these bills without the hard work of
our Republican conference. And I want
to thank them for all they have done in
this Congress, and I have no doubt that
we will continue to work just as hard
through the end of this year and into
the 120th Congress as well.
In that first speech I gave as leader
last January, I talked about my leadership style, specifically some of the lessons I learned from playing sports. In
sports you need to learn from your
losses and then move on to the next
game. As I said, part of our meeting
yesterday was a conversation about
Republicans’ future. I expect those conversations to continue, and for all Republican Senators to play a role in
building up our party.
We will see where we need to improve, identify our strengths, and use

VerDate Sep 11 2014

01:52 Nov 12, 2026

Jkt 079060

them to best serve the American people. Whether we are in the majority or
the minority, we work for the American people.
Last week, voters sent a clear message, and a large part of that message
has to do with the cost of living. Hardworking people have faced cost of living challenges for more than 5 years
now since the early days of the Biden
administration. And while I am proud
of accomplishments like the Working
Families Tax Cuts, there is more to do.
In the coming months and years, we
will see legislation like the Working
Families Tax Cuts and the ROAD to
Housing Act continue to bear fruit, and
affordability will continue to be at the
top of our priority list here in the Senate and not just next year—right now.
This week, the Senate has advanced
bipartisan permitting reform legislation. A more efficient permitting system would reduce costly delays, get
things built faster, and provide more
affordable and accessible energy across
the country. It would also encourage
investment in America, create jobs,
and boost made-in-America manufacturing.
The need is real and the opportunity
is ripe for us to get this done. We have
a bipartisan agreement that has support from the President. We should
waste no time delivering this win for
the American people. And the sooner
we finish permitting, the more time we
will have for additional priorities.
As I said, we will also be confirming
more of the President’s nominees before the end of the year, and I expect
that we will continue to face obstruction from Democrats in our efforts. But
we are going to put in the work to
overcome that obstruction to get qualified individuals in place.
Over the last 22 months we have been
busy. And I hope everyone is ready to
run through the tape because there is
more work to be done in the 119th Congress, and we are going to leave it all
on the field.
VETERANS DAY

Mr. President, before I close, I want
to say a few words about Veterans Day.
Last month I had the opportunity to
attend two deployment ceremonies
back home in South Dakota. In this
250th year of our country’s independence, these ceremonies held a special
significance. Celebrating 250 years of
American independence would be incomplete without acknowledging that
this Nation endures because Americans
have answered and continue to answer
the call to serve.
For many Americans, serving our
country is a family tradition they
trace back through generations. Others
are the first in their family to put on
the uniform. All—all—are ready to give
their lives and their future for this
country’s future.
I am grateful to all of the Americans
who have served our country in uniform. To Chairman MORAN and members of the Veterans Affairs Committee, thank you for all of your work

of all of America’s veterans. And I
want to wish all of those who have

BANKS). The Senator from Kansas.
VETERANS DAY

Mr. MORAN. We are here on Veterans Day, Veterans Day 2026. I can’t
think of an instance in which I have
not been with veterans at home in Kansas on this day, but I am pleased that
the U.S. Senate is working its business.
But I would not want this day to pass
without making remarks to those veterans that I admire, respect, and know
back home and really to our Nation’s
veterans across the country.
This is the day that our Nation
comes together to recognize Veterans
Day. We honor and pay tribute to those
who have served and sacrificed to protect our freedoms. There is no group of
people that I hold in higher regard
than our Nation’s veterans. The challenges they have faced, the courage
they have shown, the patriotism they
exude, and their selfless sacrifice for
our freedoms are something we will
never be able to repay.
It is my view that I, as a citizen—all
of us, as citizens, have the obligation
to protect the freedoms and liberties
that our Founders, the Framers of our
Constitution, gave us and that these
men and women over decades, over generations, have fought to preserve.
Veterans Day was originally called
Armistice Day. This day of recognition
was created to commemorate the end
of World War I, on the 11th hour of the
11th day of the 11th month. Years later,
in 1953, Alvin J. King of Emporia—a
town in my home State of Kansas—proposed changing the name from ‘‘Armistice Day’’ to ‘‘Veterans Day’’ to recognize and honor America’s veterans
from all wars and from all conflicts. In
1954, Kansas’ very own President,
President
Eisenhower,
made
the

E:\CR\FM\G11NO6.007

S11NOPT1

jwatson on DSKGBHNXB4PROD with SENATE

S5570

change official by establishing Veterans Day.
For 107 years, our Nation has observed this holiday, first as Armistice
Day and now as Veterans Day. Each
year, we remember the men and women
who served and earned our lasting gratitude.
Today, we have a special opportunity
to remind veterans in our lives of how
grateful we are for them and to share
our appreciation for all they sacrificed
in uniform and how they continue to
serve our Nation as veterans.
My dad Ray Moran served in Italy
and Africa during World War II. While
his military service earned him the
honor that is due all veterans, the life
he modeled for me in the years after
the war embodied character and patriotism strengthened by his time and
service in the military.
Back at the time in which we were
about to dedicate the World War II Memorial here in Washington, DC, I had
the opportunity to visit a few days before the ribbons were cut, the flags
were flown, and the speeches were
given to dedicate a new memorial to
our veterans, the World War II Memorial.
I would be remiss if I didn’t remind
Americans of the value of the service of
Senator Dole certainly as a member of
the military, as a veteran, but as the
leader in the effort to create the World
War II Memorial on The Mall here in
the Nation’s Capital.
That day that I visited, I put my running shoes—my tennis shoes—on and
walked up to the World War II Memorial to see what it looked like and what
it would be in just a few days, as that
celebration was coming.
Days before that formal dedication, I
thought about it. When I saw that memorial, I found the Kansas pillar, and I
thought of my dad and many heroes
like him. I called my dad back home in
Kansas—I stepped away from the memorial with my flip phone—to say
something to my dad about being at
the memorial. In many ways, I was
lucky that he didn’t answer. The answering machine, the voicemail, picked
up, and I left a message for my dad.
I called him, and I told him: Dad, I
am at the World War II Memorial—a
memorial built in your honor—and,
Dad, I want you to know that I respect
you. Dad, I thank you for your service,
and, Dad, I love you.
I say I was lucky that the voicemail
picked up because I don’t know that I
would have said those words if I was
actually speaking to my dad.
Perhaps as an aside, on my way back
to my office here on Capitol Hill, my
phone rang, my flip phone rang. It was
my dad at home in Plainville, KS. He
said: GERALD, you left me a message. I
couldn’t understand it. Would you repeat it?
I no longer can repeat it to my dad,
but I can say it to others and will say
it here today on the floor of the U.S.
Senate.
I am not a veteran. I chair the Senate Veterans’ Committee.

When I was in high school, the Vietnam war was ongoing. If you were a
year and certainly 2 years older than
me, you served in Vietnam. And I saw
the circumstances under which those
who served in Vietnam returned to our
country, and in too many instances,
they were mistreated, disrespected. I
told myself, as a then-16-year-old kid, I
am going to do everything I can to say
thank you and express my respect for
those who served in hopes that I can
make up for or compensate for how
those who served in Vietnam were
treated. Veterans Day is a perfect day
to do that, but so are all 364 other days
of the year.
I said I was the chairman of the Veterans’ Committee. I never envisioned
in my life being the chairman of the
Veterans’ Committee because I never
envisioned in my life ever being a
Member of Congress. But I served on
the House Veterans’ Affairs Committee, and I now serve on the Senate
Veterans’ Affairs Committee with
many of the Members of this body, this
Senate, who care deeply about those
who served our Nation in the military.
Veterans Day is dedicated to honoring people like my dad and so many
others. Today, we honor all those who
have worn the uniform, and we have a
duty as Members of Congress to make
certain that they are receiving the
healthcare benefits and the services
they have earned and deserve.
I indicated to you that I made a commitment to myself that I was always
going to say ‘‘Thank you, and I respect
you,’’ and I continue to say those
things. I have done it today to those
who served in our military. But as
Members of the U.S. Senate, we have
an opportunity to do more than just
say thank you; we have an opportunity
to actually meet the requirements,
keep
our
commitments—promises
made, promises kept—to those who
served.
So today, we say thank you, but at
the same time, we recommit ourselves—the Senate Veterans’ Committee, the U.S. Senate—we recommit
ourselves to making sure that we live
up to the other obligations we have to
those who served beyond mere words.
Today, the U.S. House of Representatives is voting on landmark legislation
that would support tens of millions of
veterans and their families, their caregivers, and their survivors. This legislative package, known as Take Care of
America’s Veterans Act, includes the
Major Richard Star Act, which would
allow combat-disabled veterans to receive both their earned retirement pay
and their VA disability benefits. The
Richard Star Act would allow our disabled combat veterans to receive
both—both—their earned retirement
pay and their disability benefits.
In addition to that major piece of
legislation included in the Take Care
of America’s Veterans Act, there are a
number of items which are hugely important to many other veterans, their
families, and their caregivers.

The Take Care of America’s Veterans

Mr. President, let me say a word
about the elections and where we go
from here. Our midterm elections are
pretty much over. We are still counting
votes in some places—in particular,
Mississippi and Alaska. Republicans
lost. Democrats won. The American
people have decided.
This was not a normal midterm election. If you have concluded otherwise,
congratulations—you are stupid. If you

go back 50 years or so, it is fairly normal for the party opposing the party of
the President in power to lose votes in
Congress, even to lose Congress, lose
one or both Houses of Congress. That is
fairly common. There are two exceptions—1998 and I think 2002.
So to the extent that Republicans
have lost control of Congress, historically, that is not abnormal. It was the
size of the loss. Normally, there is
about a seven-vote swing. In this case,
under normal circumstances, it would
be a seven-vote swing in favor of the
Democrats. In this election, there was
a 13-vote swing, a 13 percent swing.
This was not a normal midterm election.
People have a multitude of reasons
for voting as they do, and a lot of people with big brains are going to be
talking about the reasons people voted
as they did. Let me say it again: People
have a multitude of reasons for voting
how they choose. And my opinion, for
what it is worth, the Democrats won
because they did a better job of acknowledging the economic pain of the
American people. They did a better job
than Republicans.
At a macrolevel, our economy is
doing OK. Last quarter, we had about
31⁄2 percent GDP growth, but we are not
doing OK at the microlevel. If you go
back 12 months, rolling 12 months
through December—this doesn’t include October numbers; things got actually worse in October—but if you go
back from September, back for 12
months, we had inflation of 3.7 percent,
and we had wage growth of 3.1 percent.
People who get up every day and go
to work and pay their taxes and obey
the law and try to do the right thing by
their kids and try to save some money
for retirement are falling behind, and
they concluded that Washington was
not listening and did not care. The Republicans were in control of Washington. And I think that is what happened.
Now, the question becomes for us
now as Republicans in the waning 50plus days of this congressional session:
What do we do? What do we do?
In January, the Democrats will take
over, but Republicans are in power
until then. The first thing we ought to
do is as Republicans still in charge is
put down our juice boxes and listen—
and listen—to the American people.
The second thing that we need to do
is stop the bleeding, pick ourselves up,
and fight. It is not unusual up here to
have both victory and defeats. To some
extent, victories and defeats in Washington are temporary. To some extent,
they are distractions.
To some extent, whether you win or
lose is a distraction. Winning is a lot
more fun, but you learn a lot from losing. And Republicans need to learn
from this loss.
Let me say it again: We need to put
down our juice boxes and listen very
carefully to the American people. I
have.
And then we need to stop the bleeding and pick ourselves up and fight. It

VETERANS’ COMPENSATION COSTOF-LIVING ADJUSTMENT ACT OF
2026
Mr. MORAN. Mr. President, just a
few moments ago—a half hour ago—I
was on the floor recognizing our veterans from across the country, recognizing the veteran Members of the U.S.
Senate, and I indicated that I would be
back on the Senate floor today to take
concrete action to demonstrate our respect and gratitude to those who
served our Nation by passing legislation that keeps our commitments, that
makes certain that our veterans have a
greater opportunity to a fuller life and
that we reward them and pay tribute to

VerDate Sep 11 2014

November 11, 2026

CONGRESSIONAL RECORD — SENATE

04:14 Nov 12, 2026

Jkt 079060

them for their service in actions, not
just words.
I am here to do that. I am joined by
a number of my colleagues in support
of this legislation and certainly in support of the veterans that it will benefit.
I also know that since my last remarks on the Senate floor—in those remarks, I mentioned the Take Care of
America’s Veterans Act, indicating it
would deliver meaningful benefits to
those who served our country. And
since that time, the House of Representatives has unanimously, by voice
vote, passed that legislation, what we
call TCAVA. And it will provide millions of veterans, caregivers, survivors,
and their families who have been waiting a long time for those benefits—it
will give them the opportunity to receive those.
I can think of no better way—I applaud the House of Representatives for
the passage of that legislation. And I
can think of no better way for our veterans to be honored today—this Veterans Day 2026—than us now taking up
that legislation and seeing it to its
final moment, signed by the President
of the United States, enacted into law,
to the benefit of many caregivers, veterans, and family members.
And, importantly, that legislation includes the Richard Star Act to end this
unjust way in which we treat those
who are disabled and reduce their compensation related to their retirement
benefits.
So significant developments that I
pay tribute to are occurring today on
this specific occasion.
But I rise now in support of legislation introduced by Ranking Member
Senator BLUMENTHAL and me and members of the Veterans’ Affairs Committee in particular. The legislation is
the Veterans’ Compensation Cost-ofLiving Adjustment, or COLA, Act. This
is a nonpartisan, commonsense bill
where Congress comes together.
I indicated in my remarks earlier
that, surely, veterans is a place where
we can still set aside partisan differences and work on behalf of every
veteran in this country. And here is an
example of that happening—bipartisan
effort, bipartisan legislation—to come
together to pass this legislation.
It makes certain the VA’s disability
compensation benefits for survivors
and dependents and others covered by
benefits keep pace with the Social Security adjustments. They are adjusted,
as is Social Security, and now these
veterans’ benefits are adjusted to keep
pace with the Consumer Price Index.
Veterans and their families deserve
peace of mind, knowing the benefits
they earned will keep pace with the
changing economy. Millions of veterans and survivors rely upon these
benefits to help make ends meet, and
this annual adjustment helps alleviate
financial uncertainty that can come
with rising costs.
Today, we honor and recognize those
who have served our Nation in uniform.

SUBMITTED RESOLUTIONS
SENATE
RESOLUTION
956—HONORING THE SERVICE AND SACRIFICE OF THE VETERANS OF
THE UNITED STATES ON VETERANS DAY, 2026
Mrs. MOODY submitted the following
resolution; which was considered and
agreed to:
S. RES. 956
Whereas President Woodrow Wilson declared November 11, 1919, as the first observance of Armistice Day, on the one-year anniversary of the end of hostilities in World War
I;
Whereas, in 1938, Congress passed a law to
establish November 11 annually as Armistice
Day to honor World War I veterans;
Whereas, following heroic actions from
servicemembers in World War II and the Korean War, Congress passed Public Law 83–380
in 1954, to change Armistice Day to Veterans
Day;
Whereas Veterans Day honors all who have
served in the United States military during
war or peacetime;
Whereas the Department of Veterans Affairs indicates that since the founding of the
country, more than 41,000,000 people of the

PO 00000

Frm 00009

Fmt 4624

Sfmt 0634

United States have earned the title of veteran;
Whereas the Department of Veterans Affairs reports that, as of the date of the introduction of this resolution, approximately
17,300,000 veterans are living;
Whereas veterans have kept the darkness
of evil from extinguishing the blessed light
of freedom;
Whereas veterans have gone forward to
fight kaisers, commissars, fascists, Nazis,
and terrorists to preserve the freedoms of
the United States;
Whereas the families of the veterans also
sacrifice greatly for the United States;
Whereas veterans must continue to receive
the services and support they rightfully deserve; and
Whereas veterans service organizations
and other groups provide critical advocacy
on behalf of the veterans of the United
States: Now, therefore, be it
Resolved, That the Senate—
(1) honors the service and sacrifice of the
veterans of the United States; and
(2) calls on the people of the United States
to observe Veterans Day in recognition of
the heroic actions of veterans to protect the
United States and allow freedom to endure.
f

SENATE
RESOLUTION
957—EXPRESSING SUPPORT FOR THE
DESIGNATION OF NOVEMBER 15,
2026, AS ‘‘NATIONAL WARRIOR
CALL DAY’’ AND RECOGNIZING
THE
IMPORTANCE
OF
CONNECTING
MEMBERS
OF
THE
ARMED FORCES AND VETERANS
IN THE UNITED STATES TO SUPPORT STRUCTURES NECESSARY
TO TRANSITION FROM THE BATTLEFIELD, ESPECIALLY PEERTO-PEER CONNECTION
Mrs. SHAHEEN (for herself, Mr. COTTON, Mr. BLUMENTHAL, Ms. HIRONO, Mr.
Mr.
BUDD,
Mr.
SULLIVAN,
HICKENLOOPER, Mr. SHEEHY, Mr. BOOKER, Ms. CORTEZ MASTO, Ms. BALDWIN,
Mr. SCHMITT, Mr. SCOTT of Florida, Ms.
ROSEN, Ms. WARREN, Mr. BENNET, Mr.
BOOZMAN, and Mr. FETTERMAN) submitted the following resolution; which
was considered and agreed to:
S. RES. 957
Whereas establishing an annual ‘‘National
Warrior Call Day’’ will draw attention to the
members of the Armed Forces and veterans
in the United States whose connection to
one another is key to supporting those members and veterans who may be dangerously
disconnected from family, friends, and support systems;
Whereas the number of suicides of members of the Armed Forces was 471 in 2024, 302
of whom were on active duty;
Whereas, in 2024, the overall rate of suicide
deaths per 100,000 members of the Armed
Forces reached 23.2;
Whereas, in 2024, there were 6,488 suicide
deaths among veterans, a slight decrease
from 6,662 suicide deaths in 2023;
Whereas the rate of suicide among veterans in 2024 was 36.4 per 100,000;
Whereas, adjusting for age differences, the
suicide rate in 2024 among female veterans
was 99.9 percent higher than for female nonveterans, and the age-adjusted rate for male
veterans was 56.5 percent higher than for
male non-veterans;
Whereas the Department of Veterans Affairs found in its 2026 National Veteran Suicide Prevention Annual Report that in 2024,
‘‘Suicide rates were highest among veterans
18 to 34.’’;

E:\CR\FM\A11NO6.004

S11NOPT1

S5576

Whereas over half of the veterans who die
by suicide have had no known contact with
the Department of Veterans Affairs in the
year they died or in the year prior to the
year they died;
Whereas a myriad of factors leads to increased isolation and disconnection, further
exacerbating mental and physical ailments
such as post-traumatic stress disorder, traumatic brain injury, anxiety, and depression;
Whereas invisible wounds linked to an underlying and undiagnosed traumatic brain
injury can present as or exacerbate a mental
health condition, a problem that can be addressed through appropriate medical treatment;
Whereas additional research is needed to
establish traumatic brain injury as a root
cause of invisible wounds and suicide by
members of the Armed Forces and veterans;
and
Whereas November 15, 2026, would be an appropriate day to designate as ‘‘National Warrior Call Day’’: Now, therefore, be it
Resolved, That the Senate—
(1) supports the designation of November
15, 2026, as ‘‘National Warrior Call Day’’;
(2) encourages all individuals in the United
States, especially members of the Armed
Forces serving on active duty and veterans,
to call a warrior, have an honest conversation, and connect them with support, understanding that making a warrior call could
reduce isolation and potentially save a life;
and
(3) implores all individuals in the United
States to recommit themselves to engaging
with members of the Armed Forces and veterans through ‘‘National Warrior Call Day’’
and other constructive efforts that result in
solutions and treatment for the invisible
scars that can afflict veterans and members
of the Armed Forces.
f

jwatson on DSKGBHNXB4PROD with SENATE

SENATE RESOLUTION 958—RECOGNIZING THE WEEK OF NOVEMBER 9 THROUGH NOVEMBER 13,
2026, AS ‘‘NATIONAL VETERANS
SMALL BUSINESS WEEK’’
Ms.
ERNST
(for
herself,
Ms.
DUCKWORTH,
Mr.
JUSTICE,
Ms.
ALSOBROOKS,
Mr.
RISCH,
Mr.
HICKENLOOPER, Ms. COLLINS, Ms. SMITH,
Mr. CRAPO, Mrs. SHAHEEN, Mr. ROUNDS,
Ms. HIRONO, Mr. MCCONNELL, Ms.
ROSEN, Mr. BUDD, Mr. SCHIFF, Mr. LEE,
Ms. CORTEZ MASTO, Mr. CASSIDY, Mr.
VAN HOLLEN, Mr. KENNEDY, Mr. REED,
Mr. TUBERVILLE, Mr. BOOKER, Mr.
TILLIS, Mr. CRAMER, Mr. SCOTT of Florida, Mr. CURTIS, Mr. MORAN, Mr.
DAINES, Mrs. FISCHER, Mr. YOUNG, Mr.
HOEVEN, Mrs. BRITT, Mr. BOOZMAN, Ms.
KLOBUCHAR, Mr. SCOTT of South Carolina, Mr. LANKFORD, Mr. WICKER, and
Mr. HAWLEY) submitted the following
resolution; which was considered and
agreed to:
S. RES. 958
Whereas the Armed Forces of the United
States train individuals with the skills, discipline, and leadership necessary to establish
and operate a successful business;
Whereas there are 1,651,542 veteran-owned
small businesses in the United States, employing 3,245,316 individuals;
Whereas veteran-owned small businesses
make up 5.3 percent of all businesses in the
United States;
Whereas veteran-owned small businesses
account for more than $952,000,000,000 in total
sales every year;
Whereas the Small Business Administration hosts events honoring National Vet-

VerDate Sep 11 2014

November 11, 2026

CONGRESSIONAL RECORD — SENATE

01:52 Nov 12, 2026

Jkt 079060

erans Small Business Week from November 9
through November 13, 2026;
Whereas the Committee on Small Business
and Entrepreneurship of the Senate celebrates National Veterans Small Business
Week during the week of November 9
through November 13, 2026; and
Whereas the week of November 9 through
November 13, 2026, would be an appropriate
time to celebrate National Veterans Small
Business Week: Now, therefore, be it
Resolved, That the Senate—
(1) recognizes the week of November 9
through November 13, 2026, as ‘‘National Veterans Small Business Week’’;
(2) supports the goals and ideals of National Veterans Small Business Week;
(3) recognizes the importance of creating
policies that promote a business-friendly environment for small business owners that is
free of unnecessary and burdensome regulations and red tape; and
(4) expresses appreciation for the continued
service to the United States by the veterans
of the United States through small business
ownership and entrepreneurship.

TEXT OF AMENDMENTS
SA 6891. Mr. JOHNSON submitted an
amendment intended to be proposed by
him to the bill H.R. 2347, to amend the
Internal Revenue Code of 1986 to exclude from gross income any damages,
other than punitive damages, received
on account of any sexual acts or sexual
contact; which was ordered to lie on
the table; as follows:

SEC. lll. GUIDANCE OUT OF DARKNESS ACT.
(a) SHORT TITLE.—This section may be

cited as the ‘‘Guidance Out Of Darkness Act’’
or the ‘‘GOOD Act’’.
(b) DEFINITIONS.—In this section:
(1) AGENCY.—The term ‘‘agency’’ has the
meaning given the term in section 551 of
title 5, United States Code.
(2) DIRECTOR.—The term ‘‘Director’’ means
the Director of the Office of Management
and Budget.
(3) GUIDANCE DOCUMENT.—
(A) IN GENERAL.—The term ‘‘guidance document’’—
(i) means an agency statement of general
applicability (other than a rule that has the
force and effect of law that was promulgated
in accordance with the notice and comment
procedures under section 553 of title 5,
United States Code, or a statutory exemption to such procedures) that—
(I) does not have the force and effect of
law; and
(II) is designated by an agency official as
setting forth—
(aa) a policy on a statutory, regulatory, or
technical issue; or
(bb) an interpretation of a statutory or
regulatory issue;
(ii) may include—
(I) a memorandum;
(II) a notice;
(III) a bulletin;
(IV) a directive;
(V) a news release;
(VI) a letter;
(VII) a blog post;
(VIII) a no-action letter;
(IX) a speech by an agency official; and
(X) any combination of the items described
in subclauses (I) through (IX); and
(iii) does not include—
(I) any information, policy, rule, or record
relating to agency management or personnel; or
(II) any information, policy, rule, or record
of agency organization, procedure, or practice that does not substantially affect the
rights or obligations of non-agency parties.
(B) RULE OF CONSTRUCTION.—The term
‘‘guidance document’’—
(i) shall be construed broadly to effectuate
the purpose and intent of this section; and
(ii) shall not be limited to the items described in subparagraph (A)(ii).
(c) PUBLICATION OF GUIDANCE DOCUMENTS
ON THE INTERNET.—

E:\CR\FM\A11NO6.008

S11NOPT1

jwatson on DSKGBHNXB4PROD with SENATE

November 11, 2026

(1) IN GENERAL.—Subject to subsection (e),
on the date on which an agency issues a
guidance document, the agency shall publish
the guidance document in accordance with
the requirements under subsection (d).
(2) PREVIOUSLY ISSUED GUIDANCE DOCUMENTS.—Subject to subsection (e), not later
than 180 days after the date of enactment of
this section, each agency shall publish, in accordance with the requirements under subsection (d), any guidance document issued by
that agency that is in effect on that date.
(d) SINGLE LOCATION.—
(1) IN GENERAL.—All guidance documents
published under subsection (c) by an agency
shall be published in a single location on an
internet website designated by the Director
under paragraph (5) of this subsection.
(2) AGENCY INTERNET WEBSITES.—Each
agency shall, for guidance documents published by the agency under subsection (c),
publish a hyperlink on the principal internet
website of the agency that provides access to
the guidance documents at the location described in paragraph (1) of this subsection.
(3) ORGANIZATION.—
(A) IN GENERAL.—The guidance documents
described in paragraph (1) shall be—
(i) categorized as guidance documents; and
(ii) further divided into subcategories as
appropriate.
(B) AGENCY INTERNET WEBSITES.—The
hyperlinks described in paragraph (2) shall
be prominently displayed on the principal
internet website of the agency.
(4) FORMAT OF GUIDANCE DOCUMENTS.—Each
guidance document published under subsection (c) shall be made available as an
open Government data asset (as defined in
section 3502 of title 44, United States Code)
and, to the extent practicable, optimized for
search engines.
(5) DESIGNATION.—Not later than 90 days
after the date of enactment of this section,
the Director shall designate an internet
website on which guidance documents shall
be published under subsection (c).
(e) DOCUMENTS AND INFORMATION EXEMPT
FROM DISCLOSURE UNDER FOIA.—If a guidance document issued by an agency is a document that is exempt from disclosure under
section 552(b) of title 5, United States Code
(commonly known as the ‘‘Freedom of Information Act’’), or contains information that
is exempt from disclosure under such section
552(b), that document or information, as the
case may be, shall not be subject to the requirements under this section.
(f) RESCINDED GUIDANCE DOCUMENTS.—On
the date on which a guidance document
issued by an agency is rescinded, or, in the
case of a guidance document that is rescinded pursuant to a court order, not later
than the date on which the order is entered,
the agency shall, at the location described in
subsection (d)(1)—
(1) maintain the rescinded guidance document; and
(2) indicate—
(A) that the guidance document is rescinded;
(B) if the guidance document was rescinded
pursuant to a court order, the case number
of the case in which the order was entered;
and
(C) the date on which the guidance document was rescinded.
(g) RULES OF CONSTRUCTION.—
(1) VALIDITY OF GUIDANCE DOCUMENTS.—
Nothing in this section shall be construed to
mean that noncompliance with any provision
of this section affects or otherwise impacts
the validity of any guidance document.
(2) CONGRESSIONAL REVIEW OF GUIDANCE
DOCUMENTS.—Nothing in this section shall be
construed to affect or otherwise impact
whether a guidance document is subject to

01:52 Nov 12, 2026

later than 5 years after the date of enactment of this section, the Comptroller General shall submit to the Committee on Oversight and Government Reform of the House
of Representatives and the Committee on
Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs of the Senate a report on the compliance of agencies with this section.

SEC. 1202. CLEAN WATER ACT PERMITTING AND

Section 401 of the Federal Water Pollution

‘‘(e) CERTIFICATION PROCEDURES, REQUIREMENTS, AND LIMITATIONS.—
‘‘(1) EFFECT OF CERTAIN CERTIFICATION ACTIONS.—
‘‘(A) CERTIFICATION OR WAIVER.—If a certifying authority grants a certification application or if a certifying authority waives the
certification requirement for a certification
application, the agency with jurisdiction
over the Federal license or permit for which
the certification application was made shall
consider water quality requirements satisfied for the purpose of considering the application for the Federal license or permit.
‘‘(B) CERTIFICATION WITH CONDITIONS.—If a
certifying authority grants a certification
application with conditions, the agency with
jurisdiction over the Federal license or permit for which the certification application
was made shall consider water quality requirements satisfied for the purpose of considering the application for the Federal license or permit, subject to the conditions included in the certification.
‘‘(2) SCOPE OF CERTIFICATION.—
‘‘(A) IN GENERAL.—The scope of a certification provided under this section shall be
limited to water quality.
‘‘(B) SAVINGS PROVISION.—Nothing in this
paragraph allows a Federal licensing or permitting agency to refuse to include conditions from a certifying authority in a Federal license or permit if the certifying authority determines the conditions are necessary to ensure compliance with water quality requirements.
‘‘(3) REQUIRED PROCEDURES.—
‘‘(A) NOTICE AND HEARINGS.—Taking into
account State or Tribal law, as appropriate,
and the review period described in paragraph
(4), each certifying authority shall establish
procedures for—
‘‘(i) public notice in the case of all certification applications;
‘‘(ii) to the extent the certifying authority
determines to be appropriate, public hearings in connection with specific certification
applications; and
‘‘(iii) a prefiling meeting as described in
subparagraph (B).
‘‘(B) PREFILING MEETING.—
‘‘(i) REQUEST.—Before submitting a certification application, the prospective applicant
may request a prefiling meeting with the
certifying authority—
‘‘(I) to ensure that the certifying authority
receives early notification of projects for
which a certification under subsection (a) is
necessary; and
‘‘(II) to discuss informational needs with
the certifying authority before submitting
the application.
‘‘(ii) RESPONSE REQUIRED.—If a prospective
applicant requests a prefiling meeting with a

the certifying authority compliance with
water quality requirements.
‘‘(II) REQUIREMENT.—In promulgating regulations under subclause (I), a certifying authority shall only base the determination regarding what additional materials are needed on what can reasonably be expected of an
applicant at the time at which a certification application is submitted.

Beginning on or after December 31, 2027, if a
certifying authority fails to promulgate regulations under subclause (I), a certification
application to that certifying authority shall
be considered complete if the certification
application includes all of the items described in subclauses (I) through (III) of
clause (ii).

2027, a certifying authority may require materials beyond the materials described in
subclauses (I) through (III) of clause (ii)
without promulgating regulations under subclause (I) through a single written notice applicable to all applicants submitting certification applications if the certifying author-

‘‘(iii) if the denial is due to insufficient information, a description of the specific water
quality data or information, if any, that
would be needed to evaluate whether granting certification will comply with water
quality requirements.

‘‘(A) IN GENERAL.—Subject to subparagraph

‘‘(C) CERTIFICATION APPLICATIONS.—
‘‘(i) APPLICATION IN WRITING.—A certification application shall be made in writing

‘‘(ii) REQUIREMENTS FOR COMPLETE APPLICATION.—A complete certification application
shall consist of the following:
‘‘(I) Identification of each applicant for the
Federal license or permit with respect to

‘‘(dd) a description of means that may be
used to monitor, control, or manage any
such discharge; and

related to the requirement for a certification
under subsection (a), and any approval or denial of such an authorization already received.

the additional materials shall only be required with respect to certification applications submitted after the date on which the
regulation described in that clause has been
promulgated.

‘‘(I) IN GENERAL.—A certifying authority
may promulgate regulations establishing
materials to be required in completed certification applications in addition to the materials described in subclauses (I) through (III)
of clause (ii) that the certifying authority
determines are necessary to demonstrate to
the certifying authority compliance with
water quality requirements.
‘‘(II) REQUIREMENT.—In promulgating regulations under subclause (I), a certifying authority shall only base the determination regarding what additional materials are needed on what can reasonably be expected of an
applicant at the time at which a certification application is submitted.

‘‘(III) EFFECT OF FAILURE TO PROMULGATE.—
Beginning on or after December 31, 2027, if a
certifying authority fails to promulgate regulations under subclause (I), a certification
application to that certifying authority shall
be considered complete if the certification
application includes all of the items described in subclauses (I) through (III) of
clause (ii).
‘‘(IV) TRANSITION.—Prior to December 31,
2027, a certifying authority may require materials beyond the materials described in
subclauses (I) through (III) of clause (ii)
without promulgating regulations under subclause (I) through a single written notice applicable to all applicants submitting certification applications if the certifying authority determines that those additional materials are necessary to demonstrate to the
certifying authority compliance with water
quality requirements and the additional materials are those that can be reasonably expected of an applicant at the time at which
a certification application is submitted.
‘‘(iv) ACKNOWLEDGMENT OF RECEIPT.—Not
later than 3 days after the date on which an
applicant submits to a certifying authority a
certification application for an individual
Federal license or permit, the certifying authority shall acknowledge receipt of the certification application in writing.
‘‘(v) NOTIFICATION OF COMPLETE APPLICATION OR IDENTIFICATION OF MISSING MATERIALS.—Not later than 90 days after the date
on which a certifying authority acknowledges receipt of a certification application
under clause (iv), the certifying authority
shall—
‘‘(I) provide the applicant a written notification that the certification application is
complete; or
‘‘(II) identify to the applicant in writing
all specific additional materials or information described in clauses (ii) and, if applicable, (iii) necessary for the certification application to be considered complete, including,
for each such additional piece of material or
information, a description of the water quality requirement for which the additional material or information is needed in order to
evaluate compliance with that water quality
requirement.
‘‘(vi) PETITION TO COURT.—
‘‘(I) RIGHT TO PETITION.—An applicant may
petition for judicial review of an alleged failure by a certifying authority to act in accordance with clauses (ii) through (v) by filing a written petition in a court of competent jurisdiction.
‘‘(II) CONSIDERATIONS.—In reviewing a petition under this subclause, a reviewing court
shall consider—
‘‘(aa) the administrative record, which
shall consist of the materials required to be
submitted under clause (ii);
‘‘(bb) any materials or information identified by certifying authority as missing pursuant to a notification under clause (v)(II);
‘‘(cc) the information submitted by the applicant to the certifying authority; and
‘‘(dd) any evidence presented to the court
by the applicant that suggests that the defense of the certifying authority to the petition is pretextual.
‘‘(III) COURT ORDER.—If a court, in reviewing a petition under this clause, finds that a
certifying authority has failed to act in accordance with any of clauses (ii), (iv), and
(v), the court shall provide injunctive relief,
as appropriate, to give effect to this section.

authority denies a certification application
for an individual license or permit, the certifying authority shall provide to the applicant and the applicable Federal licensing or
permitting agency—

‘‘(A) IN GENERAL.—Subject to subparagraph

‘‘(ii) the certifying authority concurs, in
writing, with the extension.

may, at any time during the reasonable period of time described in paragraph (4), affirmatively waive the requirement for a certification under subsection (a).

‘‘(g) SPECIAL RULE FOR CERTAIN CERTIFICATIONS.—
‘‘(1) DEFINITION OF INTERSTATE TRANSMISSION LINE PROJECT.—In this subsection,

the Federal Power Act (16 U.S.C. 824o(a))).

that such direct point source discharge complies with water quality requirements or a
waiver pursuant to this section.

307, the certifying authority shall so certify,
except that such a certification shall not
satisfy section 511(c).

this subsection, which facility or activity is
not subject to a Federal operating license or
permit, the licensee or permittee shall provide an opportunity for the certifying authority to review the manner in which the
facility or activity shall be operated or conducted for the purposes of assuring that applicable effluent limitations or other limitations or other applicable water quality requirements will not be violated;
‘‘(B) on notification by the certifying authority that the operation of any federally
licensed or permitted facility or activity described in subparagraph (A) will violate applicable effluent limitations or other limitations or other water quality requirements,
the applicable Federal agency may, after

‘‘(5) REMEDY.—
‘‘(A) IN GENERAL.—Except as provided in
subparagraphs (B) and (D) and notwithstanding any other provision of law, if, after
review on the merits of a claim, a court determines that the environmental review for
an authorization was not conducted in accordance with the requirements of this Act,
the only remedy the court may order to redress that violation is to remand, without
vacatur or issuance of an injunction, the applicable authorization to the agency with—
‘‘(i) specific instructions to correct errors
or deficiencies found by the court; and
‘‘(ii) a reasonable schedule and deadline,
which shall be enforced by the court, for the
agency to act on remand, which shall not exceed 180 days after the date on which the
order of the court was entered, except that
the court may extend the deadline once, by
not more than 90 days, for good cause shown.
‘‘(B) INJUNCTIONS PENDING REMAND.—
‘‘(i) IN GENERAL.—Notwithstanding subparagraph (A) and subject to clause (ii), if a
court makes a determination under subparagraph (A), the court may enjoin the applicable authorization, or the activity that is the
subject of the authorization, until the agency completes the actions required on remand, if—
‘‘(I) the court finds that—
‘‘(aa) the authorization or the activity
that is the subject of the authorization will
cause a reasonably foreseeable adverse environmental effect or preclude the consideration of reasonable alternatives; or
‘‘(bb) the agency failed to prepare an environmental document required under this Act
for the proposed agency action that the authorization implements;
‘‘(II) the plaintiff will suffer irreparable
harm in the absence of an injunction;
‘‘(III) the balance of equities favors the
plaintiff; and
‘‘(IV) an injunction is in the public interest.
‘‘(ii) DURATION OF INJUNCTION.—An injunction issued by a court under clause (i) shall
last no longer than the time the court finds
is reasonably necessary—
‘‘(I) for the completion of any environmental review required under this Act; and
‘‘(II) to prevent the irreparable harm described in clause (i)(II).
‘‘(iii) SCOPE OF INJUNCTION.—
‘‘(I) IN GENERAL.—Except as provided in
subclause (II), an injunction issued by a
court under clause (i) shall be limited to the
portion of the agency action that has not
been adequately addressed through an environmental review under this Act.
‘‘(II) EXCEPTION.—If a portion of an agency
action described in subclause (I) is not severable from the action as a whole, a court may
enjoin such agency action in its entirety.
‘‘(iv) IRREPARABLE HARM.—A finding by a
court under subclause (I)(aa) of clause (i)
shall satisfy the requirement under subclause (II) of that clause.
‘‘(C) VIOLATIONS OF OTHER LAW.—
‘‘(i) IN GENERAL.—Subparagraph (A) and
paragraph (6) shall apply only to a violation
of the requirements of this Act.
‘‘(ii) LIMITATION.—Nothing in subparagraph
(A) or paragraph (6) limits the remedies
available under any other Federal law for a
violation of that Federal law that a court
finds with respect to a claim within its jurisdiction under paragraph (2).

‘‘(D) FAILURE TO ACT ON REMAND OR INADEQUATE REMAND.—If an agency does not complete the actions required on remand by the

(A)(ii), including any extension, or if the

do not correct the errors or deficiencies

‘‘(i) paragraph (6) shall cease to apply to
the authorization;
‘‘(ii) on motion of any party, the court
may grant such other relief as the court determines appropriate, until the agency completes the actions required on remand;
‘‘(iii) the court shall retain jurisdiction to

‘‘(iv) if the court finds that the failure was

the applicable Federal agency may, after

account for the time required under applicable State or Tribal law for public notice and

Mr. STEIL. Mr. Speaker, I rise today to
honor the career and service of Speaker of
the Wisconsin Assembly, Robin Vos. Robin
began his public service as a student representative on the Universities of Wisconsin
Board of Regents from 1989 to 1991. He later
served as a Racine County Board Supervisor
in 1994 before being elected to represent Wisconsin’s 63rd Assembly District. First elected
in 2004, Robin represented the 63rd District
for more than two decades and continued his
service representing the 33rd District beginning in 2025.
Robin has been a steadfast advocate for the
people of southeastern Wisconsin and a leading voice on issues that strengthen our state’s
economy, communities, and future. In 2013,
Robin was elected the 75th Speaker of the
Wisconsin State Assembly, becoming the
longest-serving Speaker in Wisconsin history.
Throughout his tenure, he played a central
role in shaping state policy providing multiple
rounds of tax relief for Wisconsin families, advancing beneficial economic reforms, and
leading to numerous state budget surpluses.
His Speaker’s Task Forces have developed
important legislation on topics including mental
health, rural schools, Alzheimer’s and dementia, urban education, water quality, suicide prevention, and foster care. Robin previously
served as President of the National Conference of State Legislatures (NCSL) from
2019 to 2021 and later in 2023. He is the first
elected official from Wisconsin to hold this
leadership post.
As a lifelong resident of Racine County,
Robin has remained deeply connected to the

f

Nestled along Lotts Creek in the mountains
of Knott County, Cordia School has served
Appalachian families for more than 90 years.
In 1933, Alice Slone, a Knott County native
who had been sent to Cleveland, Ohio, by
Alice Lloyd to further her education, came
home to the mountains to teach and founded
the Lotts Creek Community School. A year
later she opened Cordia High School, bringing
the community together around a common
goal: education. Nearly a century later,
Cordia’s students are carrying that mission forward in extraordinary fashion.
The numbers speak for themselves.
Cordia’s tenth graders posted proficiency rates
of 71 percent in mathematics and 88 percent
in reading, far above the statewide averages
of 40 percent and 49 percent. In earning the
top spot, this school of fewer than 200 students finished ahead of some of the largest
and most celebrated high schools in the Commonwealth.
The road to the top has not been easy. Like
so many communities in southern and eastern
Kentucky, the families of Lotts Creek have endured economic hardship, and many are still
rebuilding from the devastating floods that
struck our region in July 2022. Yet in the face
of those challenges, the students of this small
mountain school outperformed every high
school in Kentucky. Their success shows that
academic excellence is not determined by geography, family income or the size of a school.
It is the result of hard work, dedicated teachers and a community that refuses to give up
on its children.
I commend Principal Deanna Wicker, the
faculty and staff, and the Knott County School
District for their unwavering commitment to
these students. I also applaud the parents and
families who have stood behind them every
step of the way. Above all, I congratulate the
students themselves. They have shown young
people across Eastern Kentucky that their
hard work matters, and that extraordinary
things can come from even the smallest
mountain communities.
Mr. Speaker, I ask my colleagues to join me
in honoring Cordia School for its outstanding
achievement. These students have earned
their place among the best in Kentucky, and I
wish them continued success in the years to
come.
f

home.

CONGRESSIONAL RECORD — Extensions of Remarks

Throughout her career, Cathy has been a
leader of many firsts. In 1997, she made history as the first openly gay elected official in
Georgia, winning a seat on the Atlanta City
Council. Just four years later, she broke another barrier when she became the first
woman elected President of the Atlanta City
Council in 2001. Her historic leadership not
only opened doors for women and members
of the LGBTQ+ community in public office but
also helped usher in a new era of representation and progress in Atlanta’s city government.
The daughter of a military veteran, Cathy
was raised with a deep appreciation for public
service and a commitment to serving her
country. Although discriminatory policies prevented her from pursuing military service as
an openly gay woman, her dedication to service never wavered. Instead, she joined the
Peace Corps, where she further developed the
values of compassion, civic responsibility, and
community engagement that would eventually
come to define her career.
Recognizing that true equality required both
representation and action, Cathy became
deeply involved in the movement for LGBTQ+
rights. Her advocacy eventually led her to
Washington, D.C., where she served as a lobbyist for the Human Rights Campaign, one of
the Nation’s leading LGBTQ+ civil rights organizations. Through this work, she gained invaluable experience, laying the foundation for
her future career in elected office.
In 1997, Cathy returned to Atlanta’s political
landscape with a determination to create
meaningful change. Her election to the Atlanta
City Council marked a turning point in the
city’s history, demonstrating that public service
should be defined not by whom one loves, but
by one’s commitment to the people they represent. As City Council President, she served
alongside another historic leader, Mayor Shirley Franklin, helping shape Atlanta’s future
during a transformative period in the city’s development.
Among Cathy’s most enduring contributions
to Atlanta is her early leadership in advancing
what would become the Atlanta BeltLine. Recognizing the potential of a bold proposal to
transform abandoned railway corridors into a
connected network of trails, parks, neighborhoods, and public transit, Cathy helped elevate the concept from an ambitious vision into
a citywide priority. Her advocacy laid critical
groundwork for one of the most significant
urban redevelopment initiatives in Atlanta’s
history.
Today, the Atlanta BeltLine stands as a testament to Cathy’s visionary leadership and
commitment to building a more connected Atlanta. Her contributions have left a lasting
mark on our city and will continue to benefit
generations to come.
Beyond her accomplishments in elected office, Cathy has remained a steadfast advocate
for equality, particularly for the LGBTQ+ community. Throughout her career, she has used
her platform to challenge discrimination and
ensure that those who have historically been
excluded have a voice in shaping their communities.
Mr. Speaker, Cathy Woolard’s legacy is one
of courage, conviction, and an unwavering belief in the promise of a more inclusive Atlanta.
She has shown us that breaking barriers is not
simply about being the first, but about ensuring that those who follow have greater opportunities to lead and succeed.

VerDate Sep 11 2014

04:56 Nov 12, 2026

Jkt 079060

On behalf of the people of Georgia’s 5th
Congressional District, I extend my deepest
gratitude to Cathy for her decades of dedicated service to our community.
I ask my colleagues to join me in thanking
Ms. Cathy Woolard and recognizing the lasting
impact she has made on Atlanta and the
countless lives she has touched.

RECOGNIZING KMI SPACE ON THE
ANNIVERSARY OF ITS HISTORIC
ORBITAL CAPTURE DEMONSTRATION

HON. JACK BERGMAN
OF MICHIGAN

IN THE HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES

Wednesday, November 11, 2026
Mr. BERGMAN. Mr. Speaker, I rise today to
recognize KMI Space, a space logistics company founded and based in Marquette, Michigan, on the occasion of its anniversary of their
groundbreaking orbital capture demonstration
performed on November 15, 2024. KMI’s
achievement was a historic milestone for
American space capabilities and a reminder of
the ingenuity Michigan businesses bring to our
Nation’s aerospace industry.
On November 4, 2024, KMI’s REACCH orbital capture technology launched to the International Space Station. 11 days later, astronaut Sunita Williams installed the technology
onto an Astrobee free-flying robot in the station’s Japanese Experiment Module, where it
successfully captured a free-flying test object
on its first calibration run. Over six sessions,
station crew members completed 172 capture
cycles, proving the technology’s potential to
advance operations in orbit and future space
logistics.
With this accomplishment, KMI became the
first private company to repeatedly capture objects in space and the first commercial entity
to capture an unprepared object in orbit. After
more than 200 days aboard the station,

E:\CR\FM\A11NO8.006

E11NOPT1

November 11, 2026

REACCH returned to Earth in May 2025
aboard SpaceX’s Dragon CRS–32 cargo
spacecraft. These demonstrations are an important step toward future free-flying space
missions and expanded capabilities in orbit.
Achievements like this do not happen by
chance. They are the product of disciplined
preparation, technical expertise, and a team
that runs toward hard problems. KMI shows
that innovative small businesses in Northern
Michigan and the Upper Peninsula can make
real contributions to American technological
advancement and help keep our Nation ahead
in space.
Mr. Speaker, I ask my colleagues to join me
in congratulating KMI on this historic accomplishment. I thank their team for representing
Michigan businesses and innovation so well
on a national scale.
