IN MEMORY OF THE HONORABLE
LUTHER H. ‘‘DUKE’’ CONYERS, JR.

HON. SANFORD D. BISHOP, JR.
OF GEORGIA

jwatson on DSKGBHNXB4PROD with REMARKS

IN THE HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES

Wednesday, November 11, 2026
Mr. BISHOP. Mr. Speaker, I rise today with
a heavy heart to pay tribute to a soldier, a
teacher, a statesman, and my dear friend, the
Honorable Luther Harold ‘‘Duke’’ Conyers, Jr.,
of Bainbridge, Georgia, who passed from labor
to reward on Saturday, November 7, 2026, at
the age of 97. A celebration of his life will be
held on Wednesday, November 11, 2026, at
1:00 p.m. at Saint Paul Missionary Baptist
Church in Bainbridge. It is fitting that an old
soldier will be laid to rest on Veterans Day.
Luther Conyers was born on a plantation in
Southwest Georgia to parents who had little
formal schooling but a boundless faith in what
education could do. They raised five sons and
expected every one of them to learn. All five
earned college degrees. Luther earned his
Bachelor of Science in Agriculture in 1948
from Georgia State Industrial College, now Savannah State University, where he met the
love of his life, Gwendolyn, who preceded him
in death. He later earned a Master’s in Education from Florida A&M University in 1961.

VerDate Sep 11 2014

04:56 Nov 12, 2026

E1195

CONGRESSIONAL RECORD — Extensions of Remarks

Jkt 079060

What his parents planted in him, he spent the
rest of his life planting in others.
His first students were soldiers. As a young
man he taught in the Institutional On-Farm
Training Program for veterans returning from
World War II. Then his own call came. He entered the United States Army in January 1951,
trained at Fort Jackson, South Carolina, and
Fort Bliss, Texas, and served 16 months and
27 days in Korea with a transportation crew.
He came home in December 1952 with the
Korean Service Medal and three Bronze Stars.
Even in a war zone he could not stop teaching. He held education classes for his fellow
soldiers between duties.
Back in Georgia, he returned to the work he
loved. He served as a substitute teacher in
Bainbridge, taught at Randolph County High
School, and in 1954 joined the faculty of Bainbridge Elementary School. In 1965 he moved
to Hutto High School to teach social studies,
and from 1970 he taught eighth and ninth
graders at Hutto Junior High School. He never
left Savannah State behind. The Conyers family was named SSU Family of the Year in
2009, Mr. and Mrs. Conyers were inducted together into the Savannah State University Hall
of Fame in 2015, and the Conyers-Kirbo Endowed Scholarship Fund they helped establish
is still opening doors for students who will
never know how far he had to walk to open
his own.
The people of Bainbridge first elected him to
the City Council in 1977, and he took the oath
of office on January 4, 1978. He kept that oath
for 37 years, retiring in December 2015 as the
longest-serving councilman in the history of
the city. He served as the Council’s liaison to
the Planning Commission, helped guide the
Georgia Municipal Association’s work on employee retirement benefits and leadership
training, and each year taught the students of
Bainbridge how their own government works.
He was inducted into the Georgia Municipal
Association Hall of Fame in 2008 and received
the Georgia Municipal Training Institute’s Certificate of Distinction in 2012. He was a faithful
member of the Rotary Club, American Legion
Post No. 502, the NAACP, and the Bainbridge-Decatur County Chamber of Commerce.
When he retired, the people of Bainbridge
gathered at the fountain behind City Hall and
gave that place his name. Mr. Speaker, think
on that. A child born on a plantation lived to
see the city of his birth carve his name in its
public square. The Luther H. Conyers, Jr.
Plaza stands there today. Children who never
met him will play beside that fountain, and
they will be walking on ground he made better.
I had the privilege of honoring Mr. Conyers
in 2015, while he could still hear the words. I
said then that he had done so much for so
many for so long, and I recalled the teaching
of Dr. Benjamin E. Mays that a living is made
by what we get, but a life is made by what we
give. By that measure, Luther Conyers lived
one of the richest lives this Congress has ever
recorded. I am grateful I did not wait to say
so. On a personal note, he was my friend. He
gave me his counsel when I needed it, his
honesty when I did not want it, and his friendship through every season. Southwest Georgia has lost a giant, and I have lost a brother.
The Apostle Paul wrote, ‘‘I have fought a
good fight, I have finished my course, I have
kept the faith’’ (2 Timothy 4:7). Luther Conyers

PO 00000

Frm 00003

Fmt 0626

Sfmt 9920

fought for his country, finished his course, and
kept faith with his family, his students, and his
city for nearly a century. Now he is reunited
with his beloved Gwendolyn, who went on
ahead to wait for him.
Mr. Speaker, I ask my colleagues to join my
wife, Vivian, and me, along with the more than
765,000 people of Georgia’s 2nd Congressional District, in extending our deepest sympathies to his daughters, Audrey and
Luthenya; his granddaughter, Leslie; and the
entire Conyers family. May they be comforted
by an abiding faith and by the knowledge that
the school bells, the council gavel, and the
waters of that fountain will keep saying his
name.
f

HONORING THE 100TH ANNIVERSARY OF IMMACULATE HEART
OF MARY CATHOLIC CHURCH

HON. SYLVIA R. GARCIA
OF TEXAS

IN THE HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES

Wednesday, November 11, 2026
Ms. GARCIA of Texas. Mr. Speaker, I rise
today to recognize the 100th anniversary of
Immaculate Heart of Mary Catholic Church in
Houston’s Magnolia Park neighborhood and
celebrate a century of faith, family, and service
to our community.
Founded in 1926 by the Missionary Oblates
of Mary Immaculate, Immaculate Heart of
Mary has been a cornerstone of Houston’s
Mexican American community for generations.
What began as a small gathering of families
grew into a beloved church that has welcomed
hardworking families seeking faith, fellowship,
and a place to call home.
For 100 years, this church has been part of
the fabric of Magnolia Park. It is where families have celebrated baptisms, weddings, and
life’s most important milestones, where neighbors have come together in difficult times, and
where our traditions and values have been
passed down to our children and grandchildren.
As the parish celebrated this special milestone on November 8, 2026, I want to congratulate Father Lucio, the clergy, parishioners, and all the families whose faith and
dedication have kept this church strong for a
century.
Mr. Speaker, I ask my colleagues to join me
in honoring the Immaculate Heart of Mary
Catholic Church and its 100 years of service
to our community. May God continue to bless
this parish and the families who call it home
for many years to come.

RECOGNIZING UNITED STATES AIR
FORCE CAPTAIN DAVID ‘‘PEDRO’’
MORENO

HON. JOHN H. RUTHERFORD
OF FLORIDA

IN THE HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES

Wednesday, November 11, 2026
Mr. RUTHERFORD. Mr. Speaker, I rise
today to recognize Captain David ‘‘Pedro’’
Moreno, who retires on Veterans Day, November 11, 2026, after more than 23 years of honorable service in the United States Air Force
and the Iowa Air National Guard.
Captain Moreno began his career as a Tactical Air Control Party Airman and completed
five combat deployments—three to Afghanistan and two to Iraq. After those years on the
battlefield, he spent eight years as a heating,
ventilation, and air conditioning craftsman
(HVAC), including supporting recovery efforts
at Tyndall Air Force Base in Panama City,
Florida after Hurricane Michael, a Category 5
storm, struck the installation in 2018. Captain
Moreno was one of the first recalled to the installation after landfall to aid with cleanup. In
his spare time, he helped veterans with roof
repairs and tree removal.
Captain Moreno later earned his commission and fulfilled a lifelong dream of becoming
a military aviator. As a combat plans officer
and MQ–9 Reaper pilot with the Iowa Air National Guard in Des Moines, he flew 103 combat missions and 46 combat-support missions,
logging 549 flight hours while providing armed
overwatch and real time intelligence.
His military honors include the Bronze Star
Medal, the Air Force Combat Action Medal,
and multiple Aerial Achievement Medals. Included with his Commendation Medal Citation
was his contribution to a combat search-andrescue mission, his leadership during a power
failure and severe weather, and his dedicated
efforts to train and mentor fellow Airmen.
Captain Moreno is a credit to the United
States Air Force and I wish him only the best
in his retirement.

the leadership and dedication of Head Coach

CELEBRATING KENNETH ‘‘KENNY’’
MOORE’S 100TH BIRTHDAY

HON. MIKE BOST
OF ILLINOIS

IN THE HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES

Wednesday, November 11, 2026
Mr. BOST. Mr. Speaker, I rise today to celebrate Mr. Kenneth ‘‘Kenny’’ Moore’s 100th
birthday, and a life lived richly in service to his
country, community, family, and friends.
Kenny was born in Murphysboro, Illinois, on
November 6, 1926, to Robert and Laura
Moore. It has been said growing up during the

E:\CR\FM\A11NO8.013

E11NOPT1

November 11, 2026

Great Depression left a lasting impact on
many Americans; Kenny was no different.
In 1944, at the age of 18, Kenny joined the
United States Navy. He served as a Seaman
First Class on the USS PC–822, escorting
ships from open sea to port off the coast of
Panama.
When Kenny was discharged following the
war, he returned to Murphysboro, where he
met and married the love of his life, Wilma
Reeves, in 1949. Shortly after marrying Wilma,
Kenny started a land clearing and excavation
business in town. In 1958, the couple purchased a small farm south of Murphysboro
where they raised their children.
Kenny and Wilma had a blessed marriage
of 76 years. He and Wilma raised six children—Beverly, Warren, Roger, Callie, Angela,
and Mark—in their loving home. Their children
would bring them eight grandchildren and 13
great-grandchildren, who Kenny and Wilma
loved dearly.
Mr. Speaker, please join me in celebrating
Kenneth Moore’s legacy and service as we
make the milestone of his 100th birthday. I
pray he and his loved ones have a wonderful
time celebrating a century of great memories.

RECOGNIZING AVEN WILLIFORD

HONORING DALLAS BERGL FOR A
LIFETIME OF SERVICE TO INDIANA

HON. RUDY YAKYM III
OF INDIANA

IN THE HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES

Wednesday, November 11, 2026
Mr. YAKYM. Mr. Speaker, I rise today to
honor Dallas Bergl, President and CEO of
INOVA Federal Credit Union, who has spent
his career helping the people of Northern Indiana build better lives. In recognition of that
work, Dallas was recently awarded the Sagamore of the Wabash, one of Indiana’s highest
honors.
For more than 25 years as INOVA’s President and CEO, Dallas has focused on helping
Hoosier families and small businesses thrive.
INOVA is still headquartered in downtown Elkhart, where it has been for more than 80
years, and under Dallas’s leadership it opened
a new branch in Mishawaka in 2023.
Dallas started at INOVA as a collections
manager and rose to become President and
CEO in 1999. Before that, he honorably
served our Nation in the United States Army
Infantry and the United States Army Honor
Guard at Fort Myer, Virginia, where he took
part in President Ronald Reagan’s Presidential
Escort and received numerous military honors.
Dallas never stopped investing in Indiana.
He carried the voice of Hoosier credit unions
to the boards of the Indiana Credit Union
League, the Credit Union National Association,
and the World Council of Credit Unions. He
served as the City of Elkhart’s ambassador to
China. In 2018, he helped organize a delegation of Hoosiers who traveled to Dominica to
assist credit unions after a devastating hurricane. His community has honored him with
the Key to the City of Elkhart and induction
into the Indiana Credit Union Hall of Fame.
Mr. Speaker, Dallas Bergl has set an example of service, integrity, and a genuine commitment to improving the lives of Hoosiers. As
he prepares to conclude his career in early
2027, and on behalf of both the United States
Congress and the people of Indiana’s 2nd
Congressional District, I am proud to honor his
incredible achievements and extend to him our
best wishes for the next chapter.

HONORING AFGHANEVAC

HON. SAM T. LICCARDO
OF CALIFORNIA

IN THE HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES

Wednesday, November 11, 2026
Mr. LICCARDO. Mr. Speaker, I rise to honor
AfghanEvac and I include in the RECORD the
following statement from the organization to
the Members of the 119th and incoming 120th
United States Congress:
We walked here this morning from the Lincoln Memorial, where each of us signed this
letter.
We are American veterans, the frontline
civilians who served beside them, and the Afghans who fought and worked alongside our
troops and diplomats for 20 years. We come
with gratitude, with hope, and with a promise we believe this country still means to
keep.
A new Congress will soon take its seats.
We see a new day: a chance to set aside the
fights of the last few years and come together on something Americans have always
believed. When people stand with us, we
stand with them.
In his Second Inaugural Address, President
Lincoln asked the nation ‘‘to care for him
who shall have borne the battle.’’ That
promise has never asked where a soldier was
born. It asks only whether he bore the battle.
Mohammad Kahn did. Born in Persia
around 1830 and raised in Afghanistan, he arrived in Boston in 1861 and, two months
later, enlisted in the 43rd New York Infantry. He fought at Malvern Hill and at Gettysburg. When a Union guard decided he did not

PO 00000

Frm 00006

Fmt 0626

Sfmt 9920

November 11, 2026

belong in the ranks and detained him with
escaped enslaved people, he spent months
finding his way back to his regiment. In 1864
he rejoined it at Spotsylvania and was shot
15 minutes later. He healed and served out
the war as a sharpshooter.
When the war ended, Mohammad Kahn had
to fight one more time, for the pension he
had earned. He waited until 1881, but in the
end the country kept its word. It recognized
that a man from Afghanistan had stood with
the United States in its hardest hour. He
rests today at Cypress Hills National Cemetery in Brooklyn; among the soldiers he
served beside.
This is how a great Nation treats its
friends. Lafayette crossed an ocean to fight
for a country that was not yet his, and two
centuries later his portrait still hangs in the
House chamber. We honor our friends. We
keep faith with them. We deliver on our commitments to them.
The Afghans who served beside us in our
longest war bore the battle too. Like Lafayette, they chose to stand with America when
it would have been safer not to. They were
interpreters on patrol, soldiers in joint units,
women in the Female Tactical Platoon, and
staff who kept our embassy running. Our
government vetted them and brought them
here, and today they are our neighbors, coworkers, small-business owners, and friends
in communities across the country. Their future here is still unsettled, and too many
live with the fear of being sent back to the
Taliban, the very enemy they helped us
fight.
Our allies do not ask for more than Mohammad Kahn received. They ask to be recognized for what they did, and to be kept
safe by the country they served. Like Kahn,
they should not have to fight for it. Unlike
Kahn, they should not have to wait 16 years.
This promise reaches beyond the people
who wore a uniform or carried a badge. Our
allies came with their families: spouses who
held homes together through every deployment, parents who were hunted because of a
son’s or daughter’s service, and children who
have now lived more of their lives in America than in Afghanistan. Others came because they were teachers, journalists, judges,
and advocates for women and girls, people
who believed in what we told them we were
building together. The United States government brought every one of them to safety. It
vetted them, welcomed them, and told them
they were safe. That was a promise too. And
it extends to the Afghans our government
moved to Camp As Sayliyah in Qatar, who
are still waiting, years later, for the country
that brought them there to finish what it
started. A promise to an ally is a promise to
the family that paid the price beside them,
to every person this country chose to bring
to safety, and to every person still waiting
for us to keep our word.
This has never belonged to one party. Republicans and Democrats wrote the laws that
brought these allies here, and Republicans
and Democrats lead the bills to protect them
now. Veterans of every political stripe
walked here together this morning, and we
are proud to stand with every member of
Congress willing to stand with our allies.
So we ask every member of the House to
sign the discharge petition for the bipartisan
Afghanistan TPS Act, H.R. 9899, and every
Senator to cosponsor S. 5308. The bill would
protect these allies while their cases are decided. And when the new Congress convenes,
we ask you to make protecting them one of
its first acts of common ground: resolving
the cases of the Afghans still waiting at
Camp As Sayliyah, ending Chief of Mission
revocations, reuniting separated families,
and restarting the Special Immigrant Visa,
refugee, and all other Enduring Welcome
pathways.

E:\CR\FM\A11NO8.025

E11NOPT1

November 11, 2026

CONGRESSIONAL RECORD — Extensions of Remarks

Lincoln told us the work is not finished.
We believe this is the Congress that can finish it, and we are ready to help. Let us strive
on, together.
With gratitude and resolve and on behalf of
the undersigned veterans, frontline civilians,
and Afghan allies of the Promise Walk.
The signatories include; Shawn VanDiver,
President and Founder of AfghanEvac; Ambassador John R. Bass; Abdul Fahim; Abdul
Hussain; Abdul Naser Sayedy; Abdullah
Ebrahemi; Abdullah Karimi; Agustin Gil;
Ahmad Asas; Ahmad Tariq; Alane Relk; Allison Spencer; Amanda Dombach; Amanda
Montepeque; Andrew Cogdell; Angela Chou;
Ann C. Brown; Anne Marie Staats; Arwa
Zakir Kakavand; Ashok Agrawal; Asif Khan

jwatson on DSKGBHNXB4PROD with REMARKS

HONORING THE VETERANS WHO
CONTINUE TO SERVE IN THE
UNITED STATES CONGRESS

HON. DARRELL ISSA
OF CALIFORNIA

IN THE HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES

Wednesday, November 11, 2026
Mr. ISSA. Mr. Speaker, on this Veterans
Day, I rise to recognize the extraordinary
Americans who have worn our Nation’s uni-

VerDate Sep 11 2014

04:56 Nov 12, 2026

Jkt 079060

form, answered the call to serve, and embodied a lifelong commitment to something
greater than themselves.
Today, I also offer a special tribute to the
veterans and military fellows who have served
in my congressional office, and to the countless veterans who traded the battlefields of
distant lands for the halls of Congress—continuing their service to our Nation in a different
uniform.
We ask young men and women to leave the
comforts of home, stand watch in dangerous
places, and endure hardship, separation, and
sacrifice, and give the best years of their lives.
When necessary, we also ask them for something else: to place their lives between this
Nation and those who would do it harm. Our
veterans unlock unimaginable potential in
themselves. Knowing this, they don’t flinch
and instead reply ‘‘send me.’’ They do so without fully knowing what their service will demand of them—or what it will deliver to and
for others. They know the freedoms we enjoy
are not guaranteed. Fundamentally, they believe that America, imperfect as she may be,
is worth defending.
For many, military service becomes a defining chapter in their lives. For others, including
those I am privileged to work alongside every
day, it is only the beginning of a lifetime devoted to our country.
In my office some veterans came to us
through military fellowship programs, allowing
them to leverage their experience and call
upon their training to shape and impact the
legislative process. Others completed their
military careers, hung up their uniforms, and
then served in a different way, in a different
mission—as congressional staff. We are so
fortunate they chose to bring the lessons and
legacy of service to the People’s House.
Their battlefield is different now. Their tools
are legislation, oversight, constituent service,
and the power of the written word. Their missions are measured not in miles patrolled or
objectives secured, but in laws strengthened,
veterans assisted, communities protected, and
opportunities created for the next generation
of Americans. Yet the oath that brought them
here remains unchanged.
That is what makes their continued service
so meaningful. Long after the deployments
have ended, the formations have dismissed,
and the uniforms have been folded away, the
sense of duty remains. The uniform may come
off, but the sense of duty we carry never
leaves us. I know this because I, too, have
worn our Nation’s uniform. I know the pride of
serving alongside fellow Americans from every
walk of life. I know the bonds that military
service creates, the sacrifices it asks of our
families, and the profound responsibility that
comes with being entrusted to defend the
Constitution of the United States.
I know that some of the finest Americans I
have ever met are those who continue to ask,
long after their military service has ended,
‘‘What more can I do for my country?’’ That
spirit lives within the walls of my congressional
office.
I see it in the veteran who stays late to ensure a fellow service member receives the
benefits they have earned. I see it in the military fellow who brings years of operational experience to the difficult questions of national
security. I see it in the staff whose military
service taught them that leadership is not
about rank or recognition, but about responsi-

PO 00000

Frm 00007

Fmt 0626

Sfmt 9920

E1199

bility to the people who depend upon you. I
see it in those who understand that behind
every defense authorization, every veterans’
benefits decision, and every vote to send
American forces into harm’s way are real people, real families, and consequences that may
last for generations.
Their experience reminds us that the decisions made in these halls reach far beyond
Washington. They reach the young soldier
standing watch thousands of miles from home.
They reach the military spouse carrying the
burdens of family life during another deployment. They reach the veteran navigating an
uncertain transition to civilian life. They reach
the families of those who never returned.
These are not abstract policy questions to the
veterans who serve as congressional staff.
They are personal. They have faces, names,
and memories attached to them. That perspective makes this institution stronger. The
staff who have served are bound by a shared
duty and privilege that transcends any political
party. The veterans within these hallowed
walls are no summer soldiers, they are true
heroes who have made a choice to serve this
great Nation not once, but twice.
Mr. Speaker, on Veterans Day, we rightly
honor courage in combat, sacrifice in service,
and devotion to duty. We should also honor
something quieter and no less profound: the
decision to keep serving when their duty has
been long fulfilled.
There is no parade for the veteran who
helps a constituent navigate the Department of
Veterans Affairs. No medal for the staff member who works tirelessly to improve the lives
of military families. No ceremony for the defense fellow whose expertise helps Congress
better understand the burdens we place upon
those in uniform. Much of this work happens
far from public view, without applause or recognition. But it matters. It matters deeply. Because the true measure of service is not found
solely in what we accomplished while wearing
the uniform. It is also found in what we choose
to do with the lives, opportunities, and freedoms that service helped preserve.
The veterans in my office are living examples of that truth. There are many monuments
honoring military service within a few square
miles of where I stand now, but if we seek a
monument to American military success—look
around you. The veterans who walk these
halls stand as living examples of what we are
so proud of today.
Each of them have my deepest gratitude. I
thank them for the sacrifices they made in uniform. I thank the families who stood beside
them and shared in those sacrifices. I thank
them for the experiences, wisdom, humility,
and integrity they bring to this institution.
Above all, I thank them for believing that their
country was worthy of their service not once,
but again.
I am proud to serve alongside them. I am
uplifted by their dedication. I am reminded
every day that America’s greatest strength has
never been its weapons, its wealth, but the
stalwart character and indomitable courage of
those who volunteer to her.
On this Veterans Day, may we remember
those who came before us, honor those who
stand watch today, and recognize those who
continue to serve in ways that do not require
a uniform.
To the veterans and military fellows of my
office, and to the veterans serving throughout

E:\CR\FM\A11NO8.028

E11NOPT1

E1200

CONGRESSIONAL RECORD — Extensions of Remarks

the Congress: The Nation remains in their
debt, their continued service is an example to
us all, and their commitment to the American
people is worthy of recognition in this House.
They answered the country’s call once—
they raised their hands and ran towards danger when the Nation said ‘‘send me.’’ When
that chapter ended, they answered it again.
I include in the RECORD the names of the
veterans who have answered the call in my
tenure:
Colin Aamot, John Cloninger, David Cook,
David A. Foley, Jr., Travis Galloway, Anthony ‘‘Tony’’ Garofano, Michael Hileman,

Mika, Michael O’Neil, Kirk Proctor, Todd

May God bless our veterans, may God
bless their families, and may God continue to
bless the United States of America.

RECOGNIZING SOPHIA AHSING

HON. BRITTANY PETTERSEN
OF COLORADO

IN THE HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES

Wednesday, November 11, 2026
Ms. PETTERSEN. Mr. Speaker, I rise today
to recognize Sophia AhSing for earning the
Arvada Wheat Ridge Service Ambassadors for
Youth Award.
Sophia has overcome many challenges
along her journey to success, demonstrating
perseverance at every step. Students who
strive to make the most of their education, like
Sophia, develop crucial skills and a work ethic
that will guide them for the rest of their lives.
This award is a testament to Sophia’s hard
work, determination, and perseverance at
Warren Tech North and is clearly just the beginning of a bright and promising future.
It is my honor to congratulate Sophia
AhSing on achieving the Arvada Wheat Ridge
Service Ambassadors for Youth Award.

SENATE COMMITTEE MEETINGS
Title IV of Senate Resolution 4,
agreed to by the Senate of February 4,
1977, calls for establishment of a system for a computerized schedule of all
meetings and hearings of Senate committees, subcommittees, joint committees, and committees of conference.
This title requires all such committees
to notify the Office of the Senate Daily
Digest—designated by the Rules Committee—of the time, place and purpose
of the meetings, when scheduled and
any cancellations or changes in the
meetings as they occur.
As an additional procedure along
with the computerization of this information, the Office of the Senate Daily
Digest will prepare this information for
printing in the Extensions of Remarks
section of the CONGRESSIONAL RECORD
on Monday and Wednesday of each
week.
Meetings scheduled for Thursday, November 12, 2026 may be found in the
Daily Digest of today’s RECORD.

The Congressional Record (USPS 087–390). The Periodicals postage
is paid at Washington, D.C. The public proceedings of each House
of Congress, as reported by the Official Reporters thereof, are
printed pursuant to directions of the Joint Committee on Printing as authorized by appropriate provisions of Title 44, United
States Code, and published for each day that one or both Houses are in session, excepting very infrequent instances when
two or more unusually small consecutive issues are printed one time. ¶ Public access to the Congressional Record is available online through
the U.S. Government Publishing Office, at www.govinfo.gov, free of charge to the user. The information is updated online each day the
Congressional Record is published. For more information, contact the GPO Customer Contact Center, U.S. Government Publishing Office.
Phone 202–512–1800, or 866–512–1800 (toll-free). E-Mail, contactcenter@gpo.gov. ¶ To place an order for any of these products, visit the U.S.
Government Online Bookstore at: bookstore.gpo.gov. Mail orders to: Superintendent of Documents, P.O. Box 979050, St. Louis, MO
63197–9000, or phone orders to 866–512–1800 (toll-free), 202–512–1800 (D.C. area), or fax to 202–512–2104. Remit check or money order, made
payable to the Superintendent of Documents, or use VISA, MasterCard, Discover, American Express, or GPO Deposit Account. ¶ Following
each session of Congress, the daily Congressional Record is revised, printed, permanently bound and sold by the Superintendent of Documents
in individual parts or by sets. ¶ With the exception of copyrighted articles, there are no restrictions on the republication of material from
the Congressional Record.
